Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template  
● Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross -
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.  
● Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
● The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60 . Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time.  
● The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
● Under the  GCF Information Disclosure Policy , project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to 
the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited 
Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to:  
fundingproposal@gcfund.org  
Please use the following name convention for the file name:  
“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name] -[Country/Region] -[YYYY/MM/DD]”

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
Madagascar has been ranked the fifth most vulnerable country to climate change in the world, and the ND -Gain index, 
which ranks the climate adaptation performance of 181 countries, has classified Madagascar’s exposure  and sensi tivity 
to climate change as high  and ability to adapt to the negative impacts of climate change as one of the least (rank 169 
out of 181 countries). Madagascar belongs to the group of least developed countries (LDCs), more than 80% of the 
population lives below the poverty level  of US$ 2 per day and 80% of the population lives in rural zones, most  of them 
in very remote areas with limited access to basic infrastructure. The poverty rate (at $1.9/day) is estimated to rise in 
2023 ; it was 74.3% in 2019, corresponding to an increase of 1.38 million people in just one year (2020).  
 
Madagascar has experienced unprecedented, excessive and consecutive droughts from 2017 to 2022, affecting crop 
production  and food and nutrition security across the country.  The southwest and the extreme south of the country are 
characterised by semi -desert environments with erratic and low rainfall of less than 800 mm per year. Thus, a change  
in average annual  precipitation , ranging from -8 to +1%, is projected by 2050 and it is predicted that there will be an 
increase in the amplitude of inter -annual rainfall variability with greater likelihood of extreme events.  This is particularly 
true in the project area, which is formed by six regions (Figure  4A, Annex 16) named Amoron’i Mania, Haute Matsiatra, 
Vatovavy, Fitovinany, Atsimo -Atsinanana, and Ihorombe in the south central and south east of the country. The great 
variability of rainfall in the project area leads to either droughts or situations where the water balance is very much in 
surplus due to heavy rains and resulting floods. Projected temperature increases in the southern regions of Madagascar


---
*Page 7*
---

A 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 3 
  
4 https://dhsprogram.com/pubs/pdf/PR131/PR131.pdf  
5 Malagasy government: Madagascar’s Intended Nationally Determined Contribution   are estimated to be between +1.2°C and +2.1°C by 2050.  In the next decade, under the RCP4.5 scenario, the number 
of days subject to extreme heat events is projected to increase from 9.2 days to 18.5 days and by 2050, the average 
number of days subject to extreme heat events will increase from 32.4 days to 54.7  days, posing extreme high risks to 
human lives.  
 
The high occurrence of climate related disasters, limited climate investments, lack of access to formal safety nets and 
markets, characterise the vulnerability of rural Malagasy smallholders in the project area. The main climate -related 
changes include: (i ) the decrease in the amount of annual precipitation, with more marked and/or more frequent periods 
of drought; (ii) a delay in the arrival of the rains, which requires adaptation (lag) in the agricultural calendars; (iii) th e 
increase d intensity of extrem e climatic phenomena (cyclones, droughts, floods, storms and strong winds); and (iv) a 
very high temporal and spatial variability in rainfall, which makes farming and water supply less predictable . Additionally, 
smallholders are frequently exposed to pest and disease outbreaks , which cause significant crop and income losses. 
Since 2010, some of the regions  most affected by drought events have been in the project area, including the Atsimo -
Atsinanana, Vatovavy and Fitovinany regions. The GHG emissions in Madagascar come predominantly from the land -
based sectors: agriculture, forestry and land use change (AFO LU) and represent 80.5% of Madagascar’s national 
emissions. The emissions are mainly from livestock (enteric fermentation and manure) and agriculture (methane from 
rice cultiva tion, poor soil management, deforestation for agricultural purposes and fertiliser application).   
 
The proposed project, Increase Resilience to Climate Change of  Smallholders Receiving the Services of the Inclusive 
Agricultural Value Chains Programme ( DEFIS+ ) has the ambition of reducing the negative impact of climate change by 
applying sustainable  and nature -based  adaptation measures  with mitigation co -benefits . Thus, DEFIS+ is built on 
positive lessons learned from past IFAD projects , and from an ongoing pro -poor agricultural investment programme, 
DEFIS, funded by IFAD and the OPEC Fund for Internation al Development (OFID) and other national financiers .  
The UN World Food Program has  described the recent humanitarian crisis in Madagascar, as the world’s first climate 
related famine  and it has been exacerbated by the Covid19 pandemic. The burden of malnutrition in the country is 
heavy with children living in DEFIS+ regions being among the most affected4. According to UNICEF, the proportion of 
under -five Malagasy children, suffering from acute malnutrition, which has been declining from 1992 to 2018, is now 
increasing due to the  recent famine caused by the negative impact of climate change . Small -scale farming households 
are among the most affected by malnutrition, driven by extreme climate events, poverty, food insecurity and insufficient 
access to basic services such as health, education and WASH. The effects of h eavy rainfalls and floods caused by 
climate change are exacerbated  by poorly planned and design ed rural roads . Sub-optimal  design  leads to fast 
degradation during floods , which massively and repeatedly reduces the access of rural people to agricultural markets 
and basic services such as education, health and social services.  Only 11% of the Malagasy population has access to 
road networks, meaning the development of agricultural businesses and livelihood resilience is hampered due to poor 
connectivity. Roads play a vital role in strengthening the socio -economic development and climate change resilience 
of isolated communities with critical connections to health, education and markets. Road infrastructure needs to be 
made more resilient in order to  enhance  the climate change resilience of local people through improved accessibility.  
The project area is facing significant adaptation challenges that require urgent technical, financial and institutional 
support to: (i) mitigate the negative effects of climate change; (ii) improve the resilience of human population in the  
rural areas; (iii) improve the agricultural value chain production, food security and nutrition of smallholder farmers  and 
their communities ; (iv) make roads more flood resilient; (v) improve  livelihood options and convert current production 
systems that lead to deforest ation, land degradation and use of grey technology  into nature -based and nature 
conserving green technologies ; (vi) improve sustainable water management; and (vii) support systems and climate 
resilient  infrastructures to facilitate access to markets and other social services to boost people’s adaptive capacities 
and increase resilience to climate change.  
 
The objectives of DEFIS+ are to strengthen the resilience of agricultural production systems and vulnerable marginal 
communities to the impacts of climate change while contributing to enhance mitigation co -benefit s such as GHG 
emission reduction , promoting intensive/improved rice farming techniques (SRI/SRA) , and implementing Climate Smart 
Agriculture (CSA).5 Thus, DEFIS+ will be organised around the following three complementary outcomes , which are 
strategically conceived to contribute to climate change adaptation with mitigation  co-benefit s:  
 
Outcome 1: Strengthened climate resilience of agricultural production systems through enhancement and 
maintenance of ecosystem services by: (i) improving access to efficient water management systems for sustainable 
agriculture, which includes enhancing the resilience of 15,000 ha of irrigated schemes and 8,100 ha of sub -watersheds 
through nature -based flood protection and ecosystem -based approaches  to restore 5,500 ha of landscape; (ii)


---
*Page 8*
---

A 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 4 
 improving access to regular and appropriate agro -climatic information for weather forecast and disaster risk reduction 
using automated agro -meteorological  stations for flood and drought monitoring;  (iii) improving the climate resilience of 
food production systems for 45,000 small family farms through the promotion of climate resilient farming technologies 
and practices , water conserva tion and storage technique s and integrated pest management techniques ; and (iv) 
promot ing resilient fodder production , CSA  and agroforestry good practices . 
 
Outcome 2: Increased income generation through improved market accessibility. This outcome revolves around 
promoting green roads  for water approach , supporting the climate resilience of rural infrastructure for access to markets 
and utilising the water harvesting potential of roads.  This includes: (i) investing in reinforcing works including well-
designed  cross drainage systems on 540 km of rural roads to protect the roads from damage due to climate hazards, 
such as  heavy rainfall  and floods and to harvest water f or drought resilience by efficiently managing the runoff water 
from catchments upstream of roads  with water retention and artificial recharge infrastructure associated with roads ; (ii) 
training 2,000 trainers, construction workers and technicians  on construction and maintenance standards that build 
upon a labour -intensive construction approach, which will be more sustainable in the long -term due to the reliance on 
local green technology;  and (iii) improving climate resilience by introducing water harvesting options for increased 
water availability for farming, soil moisture improvement and aquifer re charge.  
 
Outcome 3: Improved food and nutrition security through managing knowledge on food production systems. Local 
skills and knowledge will be leveraged for instance, through the development of operations manuals  for Farmer Field 
Schools , in order to scale up innovations  such as stress tolerant seed varieties and CSA practices .  Outcome 3 will also 
promote non -timber forest products (NTFPs) and neglected and underutilised climate -tolerant food species (NUS), as 
a strategy to adapt to climate change, improve agri cultural diversification, livelihoods and food security. The 
technologies that will be disseminated will not only help respond to the climate change challenge  but will also contribute 
to reducing women’s workload and time burden during farm and off -farm activities, with a positive impact on their health, 
education and those of their children. This outcome is inherently linked to Outcomes 1 and 2 as it benefits from the 
introduction of climate -smart practices and from increased income generation, both of which result in enhanced food 
and nutrition security and mitigate the negative impact of extreme climate events.  
The DEFIS+ proposal aligns well with both the NDC priorities, and the GCF Sector Guide on climate change, agriculture 
and food security, especially regarding  the investment pathway on CSA, which focuses on adaptation in agriculture. It 
is also aligned with the GCF Sector Guide on Ecosystems and Ecosystems Services through the pathway on 
landscape -level protection and restoration to maintain the ability to mitigate e missions  (project co-benefit) . Also, 
DEFIS+ aligns with the GCF Sector Guide on Water Security  through the pathway on s trengthening integrated water 
resources management.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION  
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
Climate baseline.  Madagascar's geographical location, maritime influence and wind regime result in great 
climatic diversity. Madagascar has two main seasons: a hot, rainy season (November to April), with maximum 
rainfall received between December and January, and a cooler,  dry season (May to October), which has less 
rainfall received between September and October. During the dry period, light rainfall is mostly restricted to the 
southern and eastern coasts.6 The eastern coast is hot and humid and receives between 1700mm and 3700mm 
of rainfall per year. The highlands enjoy a tropical climate with cold winters and average rainfall ranging from 
900mm to 1500mm per year. The southwest and the extreme south are characterised by semi -arid environments 
with erratic and low rainfall of less than 800 mm per year. Temperatures vary significantly across the country, due 
to topographic differences between the west and east coasts. Average annual temperatures range from 23°C – 
27°C along the coast and from 16°C – 19°C in the highl ands.7 Temperatures are the highest on the west coast, 
reaching 36°C between October and November.  
Temperature trends.  The University of Cape Town estimates that the average temperature  in Madagascar 
increased from 21.5 °C to 22.4°C from 1961 to 2005.8 Another estimate by Razafitsalama et al. (2018)9 indicates 
that the average  annual temperature linearly increased at a rate of about 0.2°C per decade from 1961 to 2017. 
Additionally, as shown in Figure 1, average temperature trends have increased from 22°C to 23°C from 1970 to 
2020.10 Comparison with other timelines from the EWEMBI climate database11 also indicates an increase in 
temperature by 0.72 - 0.84°C between 2000 and 2020 compared to the 1960 -1990 period. Finally, over the period 
1961 -2017, minimum and maximum temperatures have increased by 0.04 and 0.05°C/year, respectively.12 In 
parallel, temperature indicators show an upward trend in extreme heat waves . The highest increases of over 
0.84°C are noted in central and southern Madagascar (Figure 2).  
Figure 1: Madagascar observed annual temperature trends from 1970 to 202013

Precipitation trends. Even though data at the national level indicate no obvious trend in annual average 
precipitation, rainfall has changed significantly across different regions. It has increased in some parts and 
decreased in others over the last decades, in parallel with an increased length of dry spells (Figures 3 and 4B).15

In contrast to the rainy season, the dry season is characterised by low rainfall during May  to September, especially 
in the regions of Amoron'i Mania, Haute Matsiatra and Ihorombe. La Niña brings about the possibility of slightly 
increased  precipitation during the wet season in the western part of Amoron'i Mania and Haute Matsiatra and a 
normal season in Vatovavy, Fitovinany and Atsimo Atsinanana. El Niño contributes to the risk of drought in 
Amiron'i Mania, Upper Matsiatra and Ihorombe and the southern parts of Ats imo Atsinanana, Vatovavy and 
Fitovinany as well as to a slightly wet ter season on the  coast of the Vatovavy and Fitovinany regions and the 
north coast of the Atsimo Atsinanana region. During the 2015/2016 El Niño event, there was a 28% reduction in 
rainfall (JFM), resulting in approximately 80% yield loss for 2016. Data provided by the Nat ional Meteorological 
Agency (NMA) highlights that up to 60% of the south regularly faces water shortages, which have experienced a 
marked increase, both in frequency and length, since the beginning of the century. Furthermore, rainfall patterns 
have become  more erratic and extreme rain events more intense, thus exacerbating the risk of floods and 
negatively impacting the irrigated perimeters and lowlands in Madagascar. Thus, the increased intensity of rainfall 
variability, cyclones and El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) events exacerbate the frequency and duration of 
droughts in the south of Madagascar.  
Temperature projections . Projected temperature increases in southern Madagascar are estimated to be 
between +1.2 and +2.1°C by 2050. In the Mid -Warming Scenario (RCP4.5), in the near future, the multi -model 
mean temperature is projected to increase from 0.5°C (HC) to 0.7°C (HC) i n the dry season. By mid -century, 
average temperature could increase from 1.1°C (HC) to 1.4°C (HC) in the dry season and from 1.2°C (HC) to 
1.4°C (HC) in the rainy season. Under RCP4.5, in the near future, the multi -model mean numbe r of days subject 
to extreme heat events is projected to increase from 9.2 days to 18.5 days.18 By mid -century, the average number 
of days subject to extreme heat events could increase from 32.4 days (HC) to 54.7 days (HC).

Precipitation projections . The number of days with intense rainfall (above the 95th percentile) is slightly 
increasing, whilst the maximum daily rainfall amount shows a slight downward trend with drought events from 
1.5°C on the rise (Figure 5).19 A change  in average annual precipitation, ranging from -8 to +1% is projected by 
2050  and an increase in the amplitude of inter -annual rainfall variability, i.e., greater extremes, is predicted (Annex 
2a).  
In the regions of Vatovavy, Fitovinany and Atsimo Atsinanana, a decrease in rainfall occurs mainly during the 
cool months (June, July, August and September). The inter -annual variability of precipitation manifests itself in 
two ways: in terms of annual amount and at the beginning of the season. Wet, normal and dry years can occur 
depending on weather conditions, but the proba bility of exceeding 1500 millimetres per year in the Ihorombe 
region does not exceed 20%. The projected changes in precipitation and mean temperature are depicted below 
(Figures 6, 7, 8, and 9).

Climate hazards.  From 1980 to 2010, fifty-three  (53) natural hazards - including, droughts, earthquakes, 
epidemics, floods, cyclones and extreme temperatures - affected Madagascar and caused economic damages 
of over US$1 billion. Of these, cyclone -related damage has been the most significant. High poverty rates and a 
lack of functional institutions increase vulnerability to natural and climatic hazards such as floods, droughts, 
cyclones, extreme temperatures and sea level rise.21 According to the Think Hazard Index, there is a high risk of 
 
21 https://climateknowledgeportal.worldbank.org/country/madagascar/vulnerability


---
*Page 15*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 11 
 floods, landslides, cyclones, wildfires and extreme heat in the country in the coming years.22 The great variability 
of rainfall in the DEFIS+ project area (Figure 4A ,B) leads to either droughts or situations where the water balance 
is very much in surplus (Annex 2a).23 As the groundwater storage is limited, the surplus will create runoff and is 
not available as groundwater during the dryer season.  Madagascar is subject to recurrent droughts and the 
drought phenomenon is cyclical for the southern semi -arid areas (in particular in the Vatovavy and Fitovinany 
Regions), with some areas receiving less than 400 mm of rainfall each year, and is exacerbated  by the El -Niño 
phenomenon.24 Historical records indicate that, particularly in south of Madagascar, droughts are common 
leading to famines in 1903, 1910, 1916, 1921 and 1943 among other years. Recently, Madagascar experienced 
an unprecedented climate induced famine in 201625 and 2021, following a four -year drought, the worst recorded 
in 40 years26. This drought has depleted groundwater resources as shown by GRACE data by NASA and 
integrated modelling of the water balance using MSWEP data (Figure 10).  
Climate change is the primary cause of recurrent droughts and cyclones, which are the leading causes of food 
insecurity and frequent needs for emergency assistance in the project area.27 For example, the 2017 -2018 
drought reduced agricultural production by 60% and threatened 1.3 million people with food insecurity. According 
to UNICEF, the proportion of Malagasy children under 5 suffering from malnutrition, which has been declining 
from 1 992 to 2018, is now increasing due to the recent famine. Madagascar’s economy shrank by 4.2% in 2020, 
despite a fast-growing  population. Furthermore, droughts also lead to a decline in the fertility of the natural 
environment. Degradation usually begins with a change in vegetation and with a decrease in biomass production. 
The soil, which is less protected, is subjected to the mechanical action of precipitation, which causes a 
modification of the surface conditions and the progressive destruction of the soi l. A spiral of degradation is formed, 
and without any intervention, it leads to irreversible desertification. Desertification is an environmental problem, 
exacerbated by climate change, that is inseparable from the issue of sustainable development of dryla nds. 
Droughts affecting the project area are expected to increase, disrupting yields, crops, livestock production and 
the resilience of the agricultural sector. Monitoring the occurrence of droughts for agricultural production activities 
is very important for building human and climate resilience.28  
Figure 10: Development of groundwater storage in the projected area from GRACE Data (NASA,

 storing only between 0.1% and 5% of their volume. As a result, these basement rocks are poor aquifers for 
groundwater storage and deplete quickly during drought. The measurement of groundwater storage with satellites 
(Figure 10 right) from 200 4 until 2022 shows a decline in groundwater storage from 334.3 mm to 136.6 mm for 
the south -eastern  basins of Madagascar, corresponding to a loss of groundwater of 11 mm/year. The natural 
recharge has been extracted (shown in blue , Figure 10 right ). The potential impact of the measure of colle cting 
surface runoff at road intersections, upgraded in the study area as part of the project, and using the collected 
runoff as artificial recharge for a scenario calculation since January 2016 is shown (green dotted curve). The 
potential addition by the measure can compensate for the climate induced loss in storage (red curve) and yields 
10.8 mm/year of recharge according to the scenario model which compensates for the storage loss.  
Typology of cyclones.  Due to its insular nature in the Indian Ocean, Madagascar, and the project area in 
particular, are frequently affected by tropical cyclones. Not only are tropical cyclones more frequent in this ocean 
basin, but they are also more intense than in other part s of the world. On average, 13 tropical systems with wind 
speeds exceeding 63 km/h are reported to form each year. Madagascar has one of the highest cyclone risks 
among African countries, with an average of three to four cyclones affe cting the country every year. Climate 
change is associated with a higher frequency of cyclones, resulting in lower rainfall in the project area. However, 
the intensity of such cyclones is projected to increase by 46%, along with the intensity of associated  floods. Due 
to the negative impact of climate change, the project areas and central regions (Amoron'i Mania, Haute Matsiatra, 
Ihorombe and Atsimo Atsinanana) are expected to experience more intense and frequent cyclones during the 
wet season and will be subject to more frequent flooding. On the other hand, during the dry seaso n, these same 
regions are likely to experience periods of increased water deficits. The accentuation of such extreme climatic 
events could have a strong impact on agriculture, and particularly  on rice growing, the main source of economic 
activity in the pr oject area. Increases in average tropical cyclone wind speeds and associated heavy precipitation 
are projected to result in category 4 -5 tropical cyclones ( Figure 1 2). Intense rainfall events caused by strong 
storms and tropical cyclones, coupled with poor land use practices and increasing deforestation, can lead to 
significant and damaging floods across the country and a high number of human and animal deaths. Floods 
represent the second highest impact disaster, affecting more than 300,000 people during th e 1961 -2017 period. 
Over 30 floods or heavy rainfall events have affected Madagascar in the past 30 years, killing hundreds of people 
and affecting thousands (Figure 1 2). During the passage of Tropical Cyclone Dzumazil, the water levels of the 
Matitanana River in Vohipeno increased by 5 metres and the Manapatrana River in Farafangana increased by 4 
metres causing significant flooding with extensive damage and high human casualties. During the rainy season 
of 2007 -2012, the Vatovavy and Fitovinany regions ap peared to be the most threatened by floods. The intensity 
of these hazards will be even greater due to an exacerbation of climatic extreme events such as an increase in 
the average intensity of tropical cyclones, the amplitude of storm surges and the assoc iated rainfall, particularly 
if greenhouse gas emissions remain high.29

 In the project area, other climate hazards include soil erosion and landslides. With regard to soil erosion ( Figure 
13), the effects go beyond the loss of fertile land, with an increase in pollution and sediment deposits in streams 
and rivers and in the clogging of reservoirs and dams, leading to a threat to aquatic flora and fauna (rivers, lakes 
and lagoons). Thus, the e rosion leads to a loss of fertility on arable land and therefore, a decrease in agricultural 
yields. Lavaka  is a Malagasy word meaning “ho le”, used in geomorphology to describe a spectacular form of 
erosion. It is characterised by a large, ten -meter -deep, roughly ovoid depression that narrows downstream to 
form an outlet. At this outlet, the materials spread out in the form of a cone. Lavakas  are common on the slopes 
of the central highlands of Madagascar, at high altitudes between 900mt. and 1250mt.30 
           Figure 1 2: Floods risk map                                       Figure 1 3: Soil erosion risk   
USAID, Madagascar, WRI Aqueduct 2019  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Climate Impacts and vulnerabilities. Madagascar has been ranked the fifth most vulnerable country to climate 
change in the world31. ND-Gain measured Madagascar’s exposure  and sensi tivity to climate change as high and 
ability to adapt to the negative impacts of climate change as low and classified it in the 169th place out of 181 
countries32. Being an island country by virtue of its geographical position, Madagascar is exposed to recurrent 
climate risks that greatly affect its economy, the living conditions of its population and its natural resource capital.33 
Sectors such as agriculture, water resources, livestock, human health, infrastructure, terrestrial and marine 
biodiversity and the environment are vulnerable to climate change to varying degrees . An example are the social 
infrastructures that have been destroyed (including schools, basic health centres, and dispensaries), agricultural 
infrastructures have been damaged and administrative buildings, urban roads and rural roads  have been severely 
impacted by extreme weather events, particularly cyclones, all of which are directly linked with the negative impact 
of climate change.   
Agriculture and Climate Change.  Agriculture, the backbone of Madagascar’s economy,  accounts for 20% of its 
GDP and  employs 80% of the population, with women and youth representing an important proportion. The 
agricultural sector is predominantly rain -fed, characterised by subsistence farming and based on traditional 
practices and technologies, such as slash and burn, w hich contribute to deforestation and soil erosion, further 
aggravating the sector’s climate vulnerability. Indeed, rural populations’ dependence on low -yielding rain -fed 
staple crops and their over -reliance on single commodities (most often rice) make them  extremely vulnerable to 
climate change.34 
Climate -related extreme events such as increased rainfall during the rainy season lead to increased 
sedimentation, soil erosion and siltation, which compromise the flat lowlands and lead to flooding and crop 
diseases. On the other hand, insufficient rainfa ll and increased drought conditions will result in (i) lower agricultural 
productivity, crop damage and yield reduction, (ii) higher rates of evapotranspiration, reduced humidity and 
increased soil degradation, (iii) more difficult access to drinking water ; increased climate -sensitive diseases

 (dengue, malaria, typhoid fever, etc.) and (iv) loss of biodiversity. For example, maize production is projected to 
decrease in large areas of Madagascar due to reduced rainfall .35 Extreme climatic seasonal events such as 
cyclones can destroy crops and the flooding they generate, especially in heavily deforested areas, can leave 
behind a layer of sand that makes land uncultiva table.36,37  
In irrigated systems, rice yields are expected to decline by as much as 10.98% over the next 30 years, whereas  
in non -irrigated systems, wheat will decrease by as much as 37.45% and maize by 30.68%38, representing a 
significant threat to the livelihoods and wellbeing of the rural population. Extreme climate events will exacerbate 
these projections even further.  Variation in rainfall patterns and temperatures, along with the growing intensity of 
extreme climate events, is modifying farming calendars and consequently, increasing crop failure.39 
Faced with the effects of climate change, farmers cannot adopt adaptation measures in the absence of reliable 
agro-met information. Meteo Madagascar is the natural provider of climate and agro -met services, but demand 
generally outpaces their capability to  respond in terms of spatial and temporal accuracy. The lack of reliable agro -
met information compromises farmers’ adaptive capacity to climate change. The availability of agro -met 
information would help farmers better adapt to climate change; therefore, i mproved access to agro -met 
information is essential to reduce  the climate vulnerability of rural and marginal communities in the project area.  
Food insecurity, malnutrition, and climate change . All six regions in the project area have experienced 
extreme dry episodes and the situation has caused precarious conditions (malnutrition, undernourishment) in 
recent years, following the drought related to El -Niño and several cyclones.40 In 2021, four consecutive years of 
drought reportedly resulted in one of the world’s first famines caused by climate change registered in modern 
times, with people forced to eat locusts, insects and wild animals to survive.41 Famine is the highest internationally 
recognised level of food insecurity. Climate related drought effects have also led to agricultural losses of up to 
60% in the most populated provinces42. Thus, it should be noted that famine and food insecurity are also driven 
by non -climatic direct and indirect drivers, such as sub -optimal agricultural practi ces that result in low productivity 
for the farmers as well as land degradation. These factors are addressed through the promotion of good 
agricultural practices, which include CSA. These practi ces will improve agricultural productivity at the household 
and community levels, thus improving food and nutrition security in communities.  
 
Roads  and the impact of climate change . Roads play a vital role in strengthening the socio -economic 
development and climate change resilience of isolated communities with critical connections to health, education 
and markets.  The important interaction between the road network and the river network for the study area with 
basin boundaries is show  in Figure 14 . Road networks are crossed by and aligned with rivers. Road construction 
so far has not explored and used the potential of surface runoff for harvesting. While one focus of the project is 
to improve rural road resilience towards erosion  and floods, a second innovative aspect is to use road 
infrastructure for water harvesting. The water from upstream of the road is collected by check dams along the 
road. 
Madagascar’s poorly developed railway network and limited inland waterway transportation increase the country ’s 
reliance on road transportation. Most of the roads nowadays are in very poor condition, with the majority being 
unpaved and difficult to access, especially during the rainy season. With an estimated road network of 31  640 
km, Madagascar has one of the lowest road densities in the world. In the year 2000, 1.6  % of major roads were 
exposed to river floods at least once a year. By 2080, this value is projected to increase to 2.0  % under RCP2.6.43

 Figure 14. Network of roads and river  
Hence, investments will have to be made 
to build climate -resilient road networks 
and improve the resilience of local people 
through improved accessibility. In 
particular,  floodwater is the single largest 
factor in road damage and is considered 
the prime enemy of road infrastructure. 
According to Chinowsky & Arndt (2012), 
water is responsible for 80% of damage to 
unpaved roads and 30% of damage to 
paved roads. Road damage mak es them 
unusable and increases maintenance and 
rehabilitation costs.  
Climate change will exacerbate the 
frequency and intensity of meteorological 
phenomena posing more risks for water -
related damage to roads and the 
surrounding landscape. Therefore, to turn 
the problems above into opportunities for 
beneficial water management and climate resilience, the internationally acknowledged Green Roads for Water  
(GR4W) approach will be adopted in DEFIS+. The aim of GR 4W is to transform the way roads are built and 
maintained, by incorporating water management and climate change adaptation into the design and construction 
of roads. The GR4W is a simple approach that (i) reduces water -related damage to the roads, thanks to  well-
designed cross -drainage systems, maintenance works and the promotion of seasonal roads ; (ii) minimises 
adverse impacts of roads on the surrounding landscape, such as flooding, waterlogging and land degradation ; 
and (iii) manages water beneficially, either for the benefit of roadside water users (agriculture), or by improving 
the sustainability of water resources, by reducing water scarcity and climate disaster risks. Annex 2a and 2b 
contain  an extensive analysis of climate change impacts, models and vulnerabilities of  Madagascar.  
Market, market access and climate adaptation strategies . The Global Competitiveness Index ranks the quality 
of the country’s roads at 2.2 on a scale of 1 to 7 (World Bank 2017). Farm -to-market routes are commonly unpaved 
and highly susceptible to degradation from increasing flooding and erosion.44 More than 30 floods or heavy rainfall 
events have affected Madagascar over the last 30 years. The most damaging floods were those caused by 
cyclones  Eline and Gloria, which killed 20 people, and tropical cyclone Elita, which killed 363 people and affected 
one million, while  also causing economic damages of over US$250 million.45 Improving transport mobility will 
lower disruptions in the movement of goods and people and contribute to reducing the GHG emissions of exhaust 
gases into the atmosphere as a result of reduced travel time, reduced number of operations while driving vehicles, 
stabilising speed at a level that all ows for running of vehicles at optimum consumption levels, increasing 
considerable energy efficiency and reducing the consumption of fossil fuel. Accessing basic services, such as 
schools and hospitals, is problematic  in rural areas of Madagascar .46 Lack of access to health services and care 
increases vulnerability to climate change, diseases and malnutrition. Damage to infrastructure caused by extreme 
climatic events such as heavy rain, cyclones and sea level rise, is a critical issue, especially since the population 
is expected to increase by 40% by 2030. For example, in early 2000, a series of three particula rly devastating 
cyclones affected more than a million people and caused nearly USD 85 million (equivalent to 2% of the GDP in 
2000) in damage to agricultural infrastructure.47 In terms of jobs, investments in emergency road works have 
amounted, for the last 10 years, to approximately USD 94 million.48 Damages due to floods and cyclones from 
2010 to 2015 were estimated to be 470 -490 million USD a year. Other structural economic and institutional issues 
further undermine human resilience (e.g., limited access to credit, high interest rates, obstacles to market access, 
etc.).  
Resilience to climate hazards . Addressing the aforementioned hazards in an integrated manner, as 
summarised in Tables 1 and 2 A below , is crucial to increasing  the resilience of smallholder farmers and their

 agricultural production systems to climate change in the project area. This entails the adoption of improved 
farming practices and technologies (drought and stress tolerant crop varieties, soil moisture conservation, mixed 
cropping and crop diversification , irrigation efficiency, agroforestry and afforestation, etc.),  as well as climate -
proofing basic rural infrastructure such as soil protection from erosion and climate resilient  roads. Annex 2 
provides various options for climate change adaptation for the value chains included in the DEFIS+ pro ject, those 
that are seen as the most technically feasible and cost effective are selected and pr ioritised . By enhancing 
resilience at the household level, the risk of further natural resource exploitation during “lean” periods is reduced. 
Watershed restoration and infrastructure rehabilitation will also strengthen ecosystem services, improve land 
productivity , mitigate the negative impact of climate change, and further reduce dependence on natural resources 
for economic gain.  
 
The prioritisation of the climate change adaptation options is a process that starts with the use of the Adaptation 
Framework, Annex 23.  The Adaptation Framework is a tool that uses a multi -criterion analysis system 
incorporating factors such as cost -benefit, climate -risk relevance, farmer capacities, mitigation co -benefits and 
biodiversity support when selecting adaptation options during p roject design. Its selection of the best adaptation 
measures is informed by analysis of climate change risks and impacts in the project locations. The available 
options are scored by technical teams on the ground with IFAD experts on the design teams. The prioritised 
options are the suitable ones with the higher scores in the completed tool for the project.  These options are then 
further assessed with the climate modelling results and scenarios and costed for inclusion in the project.

Target regions and the beneficiaries of the project.  The DEFIS+ project will complement and catalyse climate 
adaptation and mitigation investments in six regions (Amoron ’i Mania, Haute Matsiatra, Vatovavy, Fitovinany, 
Ihorombe and Atsimo Atsinanana, Figure 4A) and target 447,200 direct beneficiaries. These regions were 
selected based on their vulnerability to climate change, high incidence of rural poverty, food and nutrit ion 
insecurity  and high potential for agricultural development and for facilitating access to markets.  
The six regions of intervention of DEFIS+ can be grouped into two zones:  
(i) The south -east zone (Atsimo Atsinanana, Vatovavy, and Fitovinany) is characterised by a humid climate, 
high exposure to cyclones, erratic and intense rainfall; and  
(ii) The south -central highlands zone (Ihorombe, Matsiatra Ambony and Amoron’i Mania) is characterised by 
a humid climate and increasing droughts and floods.  
The design of DEFIS+ also addresses the urgent adaptation and mitigation needs of Madagascar as outlined in 
its NDC, which identifies southern Madagascar as the most vulnerable  region to climate change. At least 50% 
and 30% of DEFIS+ direct beneficiaries will be women and youth, respectively. The disaggregation of the 
beneficiaries is presented in Table 2B .

Complementarity.  DEFIS+ will complement DEFIS’s efforts to transform the climate vulnerable and low -
performing agricultural sector from its subsistence nature to a climate resilient market -oriented and profitable 
sector of the economy, which also contributes to improved fo od and nutrition security. DEFIS focuses on 
economic development through improving agricultural productivity while DEFIS+ will focus on climate resilience 
building of the communities specifically their livelihoods  and the ecosystems they depend on . Both initiatives 
(DEFIS and DEFIS+) will work hand in hand, making a decisive contribution to the attainment of the Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDG) in Madagascar.  DEFIS+ will also complement the GCF -funded project of Conservation 
International and the European Investment Bank (EIB) entitled “Sustainable Landscapes in Eastern Madagascar” 
which is implemented in the watershed not far from DEFIS regions by rehabilitati ng and climate -proofing key rural 
infrastructures and establishing synergies to further d evelop the value chains targeted by the respective projects. 
In the southeast area, DEFIS+ will complement UN Environment’s project entitled “Adapting Coastal Zone 
Management to Climate Change in Madagascar Considering Ecosystem and Livelihoods” (PAZC) in the regions 
of Vatovavy and Fitovinany by providing communities with improved farming techniques and promoting crop 
diversification and climate -smart agriculture. DEFIS+ will also scale up the results of the African Development 
Bank/GEF “Enabling Climate R esilience in the Agriculture Sector in the Southwest Region of Madagascar” project 
in the six DEFIS+ target regions. Finally, DEFIS+ will complement the GIZ -funded “Adaptation of Agricultural 
Value Chains to Climate Change” (PrAda) project by supporting th e development of value chains and the AfDB’s 
PAPAM project by replicating and scaling -up the agro -ecological production techniques of its areas of 
intervention.  
Additionality.  DEFIS+ is strategically designed to increase the climate resilience of the socio -ecological systems 
in six regions. The GCF resources will be very important for making DEFIS+ investments in the six target regions 
more resilient to climate change and for p romoting activities that will help reduce GHG  emissions and provide 
other benefits that will be discussed in this and the next section. As a result, DEFIS+ resources from the GCF will 
provide benefits for adaptation that are not possible with  just national economic development projects. Without 
the GCF ’s resources, interventions to enhance climate resilience in the six regions would not be possible.  
With GCF resources, DEFIS+ investments in the project area will also contribute to climate change adaptation by 
improving the aquifers that are critical to supporting agricultural systems. Thus, GCF resources for efficient water 
management systems and GR4W  will be critical in improving the water retention in the catchment area, thereby 
contributing to recharging aquifers. The construction of GR4W  involves environmentally friendly , simple,  and low -
cost techniques to systematically manage the run -off across t he entire catchment.49;50 The support for fuel-
efficient cooking stoves to reduce fuelwood use will also contribute to maintaining or improving groundwater 
sources and combating deforestation. Deforestation has negative impacts on the water table and surface water 
systems as it loosens soil, favours runoff, decreases the absorption of water in the so il and increases GHG 
emissions.  
Enhance national policies and strategies on climate change . DEFIS+ is consistent with and complementary 
to the government ’s policies, plans and strategies to address climate change issues, linking them with climate 
resilience, poverty reduction, food security and nutrition. Table 3 provides a summary of DEFIS+ contributions to 
the relevant policies and strategies.

energy sovereignty); Axes 4, 5 and 6 on education, 
information, green diplomacy and governance.  DEFIS+ activities will contribute to the 
implementation of these NAP strategic 
axes, in particular Axes 1, 2 and 3.

Disaster 
Management 
Policy  It is a tool of reference and guidance for decision -
making in the context of Risk and Disaster 
Management. It is a transversal, inter -sectoral and 
multi -stakeholder framework for risk management, 
both in terms of response to climate shocks, but also 
prevention and mitigation.  DEFIS+ contributes to the 
implementation of the policy by taking 
into account the climate prevention and 
mitigation dimension in project activities.

Agriculture -Livestock -Fisheries Sector in the context 
of climate change. The strategy aims to establish 
technical, social, economic and financial bases in 
order to reduce the climate vulnerability of the 
agriculture, livestock, and fisheries sectors to the 
impacts of climate change.  DEFIS+ integrates consideration of

actions.

adopted by Madagascar with a view to achieving

to the fight against climate change in

Strategy 
(Reduction of 
Emissions from 
Deforestation and 
Forest 
Degradation)  The REDD+ mechanism in Madagascar contributes 
to the reduction of 14% of GHG emissions from the 
forestry sector, through the increase of forest cover 
and the control of forest fire emissions from the 
forestry sector, also through the increase of forest 
cover and the control of deforestation and forest 
degradation in its investment areas.  DEFIS+ contributes to the 
implementation of 2 major strategic axes 
of the national strategy: (i) promotion of 
the development and efficient use of 
land and water in rural areas; (ii) 
improvement of the standard of living of 
local populations through good 
agricultural practices and the reduction 
of unsustainable fuelwood for cooking.

B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)  
Rational e: The Theory of Change diagram (Figure 15) demonstrates how the project shifts the BAU from poorly 
managed climate risks in the DEFIS+ regions to a climate resilient and sustainable approach for smallholder 
farmers  and their communities . The project’s outcomes and outputs are in -line with GCF’s adaptation goals and 
objectives.  The paradigm shifting aspects of the project will include the data driven climate -proofing of modern 
and sustainable infrastructure, water security, CSA and a focus on the last -mile en gagement of vulnerable rural 
communities. The theory of change shows that IF infrastructure is made sustainable, agricultural production  is 
made resilient, and improved water management is implemented THEN  sustainable agricultural adaptation 
practices will be in place and contribute to NDC adaptation targets BECAUSE  water, infrastructure and food 
systems will be made less vulnerable to climate shocks.  
Figure 15: Theory of Change Diagram


---
*Page 25*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 21 
 Madagascar is a low -income country that ranks 164th out of 189 countries on the Human Development Index and 
frequent ly experiences  climate hazards.51 The vast majority of the Malagasy population, estimated at 75% in 
2019, lives in extreme poverty. Food insecurity and malnutrition are also prevalent in the country. This reality is 
particularly prevalent in rural areas, where the population’s main activi ty is low -productivity subsistence 
agriculture. Moreover, the climate change vulnerability of Madagascar and its population is exacerbated by their 
high exposure to the effects of climate change that adversely affect living conditions, the environment and basic 
infrastructure.52 
The climate change vulnerability in the DEFIS+ project area is linked, inter alia, to: (i) the decrease in the 
availability and reliability of water for food and agriculture; (ii) the limited access to and degradation of irrigation 
systems; (iii) the high level of soil erosion and the degr adation of natural resources, as a consequence of 
deforestation and unsustainable slash and burn farming practices in rainfed areas; (iv) the limited availability of 
climate resilient agricultural inputs and technologies a nd relevant knowledge and adoption of farm management 
practices; (v) and the limited access to rural basic infrastructures. Thus, the vulnerability of rural people in the 
project area is further exacerbated by the underlying high incidence of extreme pover ty, reaching 87.6% of the 
population living below the absolute poverty line.53 Women and girls experience the greatest impacts of climate 
change, which amplifies existing gender inequalities and poses unique threats to their livelihoods, health and 
safety. To be able to overcome these barriers, the financial resources of the GCF wil l be used to reduce the 
vulnerability to climate change of the rural communities in the DEFIS+ six regions (Section B5).  
In view of the climate rationale detailed in the ToC, the planned activities under DEFIS+ will aim at: addressing 
the impacts of climate change risks on agricultural production, supply chain infrastructure and road infrastructure; 
increasing the availability of water for irrigation and agricultural productivity; and enhancing the resilience of 
communities living around road infrastructure to the i mpacts of climate change. This can be done through:  
● The establishment of early warning systems that provide information on the start and cessation dates, 
duration and amount of rainfall to help farmers determine their planting schedule and crop selection. This will also 
include the integration of phytosanitary surveillance, agricultural warnings, drought alerts and food and nutrition 
surveillance, in particular.  
● Strengthening awareness raising on climate change among men, women and youth, including 
policymakers (IEC and behaviour change). Climate resilience will require a national program of information, 
awareness, education and capacity building at all levels: po pulation, local authorities and decision makers.  
● Establishing a large -scale restoration program for the most threatened ecosystems. Madagascar has 
already committed to restoring four million hectares of degraded forest landscapes by 2030, with appropriate 
planning and methods.54 However, to significantly increase this commitment, international climate finance and 
this project can be secured to help support climate change adaptation activities across the country.  
● The implementation of reforestation programs for watersheds, which could fulfil a quadruple objective, 
namely: (i) the preservation of water resources while limiting erosion phenomena; (ii) the creation of climate refuge 
for endangered species; (iii) large -scale reforestation; and (iv) sequestration of GHG s. 
● Improving water infrastructure and promoting small - to medium -scale irrigation to help farmers manage 
variable and unpredictable heavy rainfall.55 This will reduce walking distance to fetching water for domestic use 
for women reducing their workloads.  
● Improving soil and water conservation strategies, upgrading production infrastructure, intensifying 
production and providing training and technical assistance to promote the adoption of new climate -friendly 
techniques to help farmers adapt to the changing climate and reduce drudgery associated with farm 
workloads.56,57 
● Scaling up the dissemination of conservation and CSA through the replication of innovative practices 
currently conducted on a small scale. This measure has co -benefits in terms of GHG emission mitigation and 
water resource conservation.

 ● The current unsustainable rain fed agricultural practices need to be transformed into a climate -resilient, 
market -oriented and profitable economic sector supported by adequate climate -resilient infrastructure and timely 
and reliable climate information. Th is will benefit the end -users and guarantee higher incomes, better food and 
nutrition security, and stable access to water and markets.  
● Developing appropriate program(s) including training technicians and workers on the GR4W approach. 
This modern, sustainable, and eco -friendly approach will be used as the basis for the rehabilitation and 
maintenance of national and rural roads, adapted to resist extreme climatic events.  
 
Building blocks. The three  key building blocks for  the paradigm shift  are as follows:  
i.Strengthen the climate resilience of production systems, through:  
a. The development of efficient water management systems, while ensuring the integrated management of 
the watersheds;  
b. Regular access to reliable -climate data to inform risks assessments and planning;  
c. Diffusion and adoption of climate resilient inputs and of new technologies and management practices that 
reduce GHG emissions . 
 
ii. Improve the climate resilience of basic rural infrastructure, particularly unpaved rural access roads with cross 
drainage systems to improve access to food markets and protect the landscape around roads with runoff water 
collection systems set up to harness water resources collected along the roadways.  
a. Improved roads will allow for increased access to markets. This is crucial for the generation of income, the 
sharing of knowledge, and the ac cess to basic services and acquisition of basic necessities. Thus, a 
modern approach to road construction and rehabilitation has a minimal carbon footprint, while BAU road 
management has a large carbon footprint.  
b. Improved roads will allow for sustainable water management, increase water availability for farming, and 
reduce the isolation of communities, which directly contributes to lowering their climate change 
vulnerability .  
c. Improved roads will lower the risk of accidents and also allow remote communities to access facilities (such 
as health care centres) especially for women and children, thus contributing to overall health (co -benefit).  
 
iii.Scale up technological innovations on climate change adaptation and knowledge management among family 
farmers and decentralised actors, for enhanced nutrition, through:  
a. Training on climate change and capacity building services;  
b. Knowledge management on the adaptation of food production systems to climate change . 
 
This building block on knowledge generation and management will reinforce the previous t hree and ensure a 
paradigm shift in the daily lives of the smallholder farmers supported by the project.  
 
The proposed approach has high transformative potential as it will combine key adaptation interventions that will 
strengthen the capacities of family farms and their production systems , create an additional positive feedback 
loop by improving the overall climate resilience of beneficiaries and contribute to reduc ing emissions.   
Barriers.  However, some barriers and risks may hinder the achievement of the outcomes and the overall goal of 
the project. Mitigation measures for such risks are already integrated into the project design.  
Barrier 1: Poor watershed management and land use planning , lead to soil erosion, siltation of irrigated perimeters 
and lowlands and disruption of road infrastructure, which results in low agricultural productivity and undermines 
the functionality of the ecosystems on the landscapes.  This will be overcome through activities under 
Outcome1 through the focus on sustainable agriculture and watershed management as well as Outcome 2 
through the GR4W approach and climate proofing of the rural infrastructure.  
Barrier 2: Limited capacity to generate and disseminate timely climate information for decision making , for 
farmers, and the  rural population  to be able to predict the changes in climate patterns in order to better plan ahead 
and adjust, shift crop calendars, adapt cultivation practices and techniques . This will be overcome through the 
activities under Outcome 1 which consists of strengthening the climate resilience of agricultural and animal


---
*Page 27*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 23 
 production systems through the improvement and maintenance of ecosystem services, and Outcome 3 which 
includes  increasing access to and adoption of technological innovations for climate change adaptation.   
Barrier 3: Limited technical expertise and financial access to invest in climate resilient technologies , which hinders 
the dissemination and access to climate smart technologies and therefore the resilience of communities and their 
livelihoods. In particular, to tackle Barriers 1, 2 and 3 the following activities are key:  
i. Exchange visits on the adaptation of food systems to climate change and the application of  sustainable farming 
practices;  
ii.  Capitalization of best practices and knowledge on climate change adaptation measures;  
iii. Installation of agro -meteorological stations and development of water, flood and drought monitoring and 
forecasting system ; 
iv.  Capacity building on access to finance and governance  (SRM & DRAEP).  
 
Barrier 4: Limited capacity and resources to improve and maintain infrastructure, which hinders rural populations 
from accessing basic services  and increases food insecurity at the regional level . 
Barrier 5: Limited awareness and skills to adopt new technologies,  which result in inefficient use of resources 
such as water and use of traditional methods and therefore low adaptive capacity of communities and their 
livelihoods . 
In particular, to tackle Barriers 4 and 5 , the following activities are key:  
i. Barrier 4 will be overcome through outcome 1, and 2 respectively, focusing on the promotion of water efficient 
technologies, sustainable GR 4W initiative, access to markets and income generation activities . 
ii. Barrier 5 will be overcome through outcome 3, by implementing modern and green sustainable technology that 
will enhance the climate change resilience of rural communities against climate shocks.  
In addition  minor barriers to th ose listed above require risk mitigation tools and contingency plans , which  are 
extensively discussed in Annex 2a (Feasibility Study).  
The assumptions that apply to the results of the proposed project and the respective activities and that would 
allow the project to succeed in its objectives are listed below:  
- Farmers are willing and able to adopt the practices promoted by DEFIS+, also because they have local and 
sustainable access to quality inputs promoted by other IFAD projects  such as DEFIS ; 
- Government entities are willing and have the necessary means, both financial and otherwise, to maintain 
roads, infrastructure, and market access;  
- There are enough buyers (local and regional) for the agricultural products of farmers;  
- Climate information systems can produce information that is relevant, accessible and appropriate for 
agricultural production. Farmers are able to use such information to improve their farming practices and 
enhance productivity.  
Policy and gender.  The DEFIS+ activities have been defined according to the climate change adaptation 
perspectives in relation to the priorities set out in the Madagascar National Climate Change Adaptation Plan 
(NCA) and the NDCs that the country has adopted following the Paris Agreement and the National Policy for the 
Fight against Climate Chan ge, which focuses on the sectors of Agriculture -Livestock -Fisheries, Water Resources, 
Public Health, Biodiversity and Forestry, Coastal Zones, Infrastructures, Management/Reduction of Climate Risks 
and Disasters. This strategic priority refers to the NDC which foresees, between 2020 and 2030, the strengthening 
and updating of multi -hazard Early Warning Systems by integrating climate hazards warnings, phytosanitary 
surveillance, crop war nings, flood and drought alerts, and food and nutrition surveillance. In addition, Table 3 
includes a list of DEFIS+’s contribution to national adaptation strategies and policies in the climate change sector.  
Gender empowerment and social inclusion is an integral part of the overall DEFIS+ and will therefore be 
mainstreamed into project activities, complemented by targeted interventions to overcome any specific barriers 
as necessary. This approach will enable p roject opportunities to be expanded to women and youth, while also 
addressing the main gaps and barriers they face to inclusion. This approach recognises the need to identify and 
address social norms that perpetuate gender empowerment and social inclusion inequalities and exacerbate

included in Annex 8 . Youth outreach will be improved by working with Technical Vocation centres  that are 
supported through FORMAPROD / PROGRES, which are IFAD financed projects in Mad agascar.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)  
Background of the project.  The DEFIS+ project will be part of a larger ongoing programme named DEFIS, 
which benefits from 40 years of IFAD presence and experience in family farming in Madagascar. DEFIS is a multi -
actor funded programme (IFAD, OFID, the government and beneficiaries)  that will scale up tested results and 
best practices focusing on the value chains identified in Annex 2  and prioritised based on the value chain studies 
(covering climate change, production potential and challenges in value chain development). DEFIS cover s two 
funding cycles for IFAD for a total of 10 years. DEFIS was designed under IFAD’s tenth replenishment cycle 
(IFAD10, 2016 -2018) which, based on country driven demands, focused on agricultural value chain development 
as the pillar of an integrated approach to rural economic development.  The first batch of financing was approved 
by the IFAD’s Executive Board (EB) in December 2017, entered into force in March 2018 and is expected to 
complete activities in 2027. In December 2021, IFA D's EB approved the sec ond batch of financing. As part of 
IFAD’s increased focus on climate resilience, in IFAD12 (2022 -2024) there is a substantial increase in the 
availability of funding for climate activities (40% of total IFAD financing) and thus the GoM requested the DEFIS+


---
*Page 29*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 25 
 project to increase climate resilience of the food production and socioecological systems in six out of nine regions 
of DEFIS.  
Complementarity.  DEFIS and DEFIS+ complement each other because DEFIS aims to improve productivity in 
select agricultural value chains, while DEFIS+ aims to increase the resilience to climate change of smallholders 
benefiting from the services of the improved agricultural value chains.  The decision that DEFIS covered nine 
regions and DEFIS+ only six out of nine (Figure 16) was taken by the National Designated Authorities and the 
GoM because the other three regions were included in other projects. Thus, to avoid overlapping with these new 
projects, it was decided to exclude them from DEFIS+.  DEFIS+ design took advantage of and capitalised on 
experiences and emerging lessons identified during the ongoing implementation and supervision of the DEFIS 
project. In addition, DEFIS+ benefitted from exchanges with other actors in rural development, pa rticularly projects 
financed by IFAD (AD2M, AROPA, FORMAPROD) and those of other development partners.  All these initiatives 
will be carried out with a view to continuous ly learning and improving performance. The inclusion of the poorest 
and most climat e change affected people in local development dynamics requires specific targeting mechanisms. 
These mechanisms for targeting vulnerable people (AROPA) and  boosting micro -projects (AD2M and 
FORMAPROD) with climate change and gender components have been tested and approved.  
In addition to complementing on -going projects and programmes , DEFIS+ is perfectly aligned with the GCF 
Sectoral Guides, as summarised in Table 4.

beneficiaries, DEFIS+ will also adopt a participatory approach that will make it possible to involve local authorities 
and representatives of Farmer Organisation (FO) beneficiaries in the identification and validation of direct and

 Currently, farmers sell very little produce due to i) extreme climate hazards that destroy large areas of crops every 
year; ii) low production levels; iii) low access to markets due to poor roads; iv) crop diseases (i.e., insects, virus es 
and infections) ; v) poor weather forecast information and, v i) inaccurate climate change predictions. The low 
production levels also limit household food consumption levels, resulting in food and nutrition insecurity. Self -
targeting will make it possible to define and vali date with the local populations the eligibility criteria based on the 
level of poverty, climate vulnerability, access to resources and markets, food insecurity and malnutrition status. 
Thus, it will facilitate the identification of poor and vulnerable bene ficiaries within the communities/villages and/or 
the area with their participation and that of the FOs.  
Geographically, the DEFIS+ project will intervene in areas with a relatively high concentration of poor and 
extremely poor people, where climate hazards are not predicted on time, food insecurity , infection diseases,  and 
malnutrition prevalence are high, and the negative effects of climate change affect the productive capital of the 
populations. Thus, specific actions proposed for women and young people in vulnerable situations will facilitate 
their access to knowledge, productive capital, income -generating  activities and green jobs.  
Expected impacts and outcomes of DEFIS+ . The expected impacts of DEFIS+ are: (i) increased resilience to 
climate -related shocks and food and nutrition insecurity and improved livelihoods of family farmers; (ii) improved 
resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services ; (iii) increased resilience of basic rural infrastructure to the 
effects of climate change; and (iv) reduced GHG emissions from forestry and land use.   
The expected outcomes are:  
● Strengthened climate resilience of agricultural production systems and increased carbon sequestration 
through: (i) the adoption of energy and water efficient technological innovations and climate smart practices that 
reduce GHG emissions,  (ii) improved and secure water availability, including the protection of sub -watersheds 
from climate shocks and the restoration of landscapes; and (ii) enhanced agro -climatic information system.  
● Increased income generation through  improved market accessibility through (i) enhanced  robustness of 
roads; (ii) training of trainers on new construction standards for small -scale, climate -resilient rural infrastructure 
for local communities; and (iii) runoff water collection systems set up to harness water resources collected along 
the road ways.  
● Improved food and nutrition security, through scaling up innovations on climate change adaptation, 
knowledge management and the exchange of best agricultural practices.  
 
The outcomes , outputs and activities of DEFIS+ are extensively discussed below, and a summary of the project 
approach is depicted in Figure 17 .

*Page 32*
