 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 28 
 Figure 17: Project’s approach  
 
 
Outcome 1: Strengthened climate resilience of agricultural production systems  
DEFIS+ will place emphasis  on the dissemination and adoption of climate smart agronomic practices, stress 
tolerant varieties, species and fodders, as well as additional investments needed to improve water use efficiency, 
and the resilience of basic infrastructure to climate shocks.  The dissemination of improved climate -friendly 
agricultural and livestock management practices  as well as energy efficiency tools  will be supported through 
Farmer Field Schools (FFS), which allows for a long -term paradigm shift towards sustainable agriculture  and 
green energy solutions . In addition, through the FFS, the project promotes the transfer of skills in improved 
cropping practices, integrated pest management, and the use of sustainable agricultural inputs.   
 
Output 1.1: Improved water management for sustainable agriculture  
In order to sustain agricultural production, certain measures are a priority. Investment in and development of 
water resource management systems (irrigation, drainage and retention) is a major issue. However, it is also 
necessary to overcome the pressures linked to co mpeting  uses, through an integrated and sustainable 
management of these resources. The objectives are to reduce climate change vulnerability in the targeted 
watersheds by strengthening the agricultural and livestock sectors  on the basis of an  Integrated Water Resources 
Management (IWRM) approach and improving governance in order to protect and enhance the natural resources 
within the limits of each watershed or sub -watershed. To achieve the IWRM foundatio n, water cycle management 
analyses will be carried out at the watershed level and will make it possible to indicate or specify the main issues 
linked to water resources, both in terms of quality and quantity. Thus, the analysis will identify the problems and 
their causes within the sub -watersheds, inform the IWRM system to be implemented and determine the scope of 
activities in priority areas to enhance water preservation mechanisms.  
The activities planned under Output 1.1, which will be undertaken using the IWRM, are the following:  
Activity 1.1.1. Protection of irrigation schemes against climate change impacts


---
*Page 33*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 29 
 A large majority of existing irrigation schemes in different regions of Madagascar and in the DEFIS+ regions were 
built by the rural engineering department or by other projects in the 1960s. The infrastructure is old and needs to 
be rehabilitated and reinf orced because of the evolution of water and soil problems linked to climate change 
(floods, erosion, cyclones, decrease in water resources during the dry season, etc.). GCF funds will therefore 
allow the financing of the rehabilitation of irrigated perimet ers and the construction of new ones with improved 
drainage systems. Thus, the regeneration of the irrigation schemes and any other activity that enables further 
water extraction will be carefully designed once the water balance is analysed without further  harm to communities 
and exacerbation of climate issues related to water scarcity. This will happen in parallel with IWRM system 
implementation. Furthermore, the rehabilitation/construction of irrigation schemes will contribute to increase d 
production and diversification (including the production of nutrient -dense foods such as cereals and legumes ) to 
improve the food and nutrition security at a household and community level with particular benefit to women and 
young children . This activity will be complemented by Activity 1.1.3 (Strengthening capacities of Water Users 
Associations  (WUAs) ) to ensure the sustainability of the investments. Special attention will be given to ensuring 
and increasing women’s access to irrigated plots through engagement with tradi tional authorities and community 
engagement to provide allocation quotas for women and youth and avoid potential backlash. In addition, a 
comprehensive water cycle management analysis will be conducted to identify the main issues linked to water 
resources,  both in terms of quality and quantity and in relation to implications for women and men, in the DEFIS+ 
regions. W UAs will be provided with small start -up and spare equipment to maintain these rehabilitated irrigated 
perimeters. However, complex constructi on will be conducted by contractors or procured by construction parties. 
Thus, procurement is carried out by the person in charge of procurement at the Ministry of Agriculture and 
Livestock, with the support of the DEFIS+ Procurement Manager.  
Activity 1.1.2. Sustainable sub -watersheds (adjacent to irrigated schemes) management and restoration of 
landscape  
This activity entails watershed development plans by the communities. The climate adaptation plans will include 
priority landscape restoration, ecosystems restoration, natural water purification, rainwater harvesting aimed at 
reducing workloads for women a nd capacity building to promote non -regret solutions. This will be complemented 
with the adoption of biological (planting herbaceous or shrub species, along curves or perpendicular to prevailing 
winds) and structural (benches, terraces, techniques to preve nt gullies and lavakas) measures to protect sub -
watersheds near irrigated perimeters from wind and water erosion, improve vegetation cover, improve water 
infiltration and reduce erosion. The plans will apply IWRM  principles, bringing together the different actors for joint 
planning. As a result, the technical departments of MINAE, the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable 
Development, the project’s  implementing partners, and representatives of the beneficiaries, farmer ’' 
organizations, the chamber of agriculture, and the technical departments concerned will hold technical workshops 
to validate the adaptation plans.  
Activity 1.1.3. Strengthening the capacities of Water Users Associations to manage water and apply sustainable 
water management practices.  
WUA members and local technicians (including building inspectors, engineers, architects and designers) will be 
trained on the Hydro -agricultural Infrastructures Against Floods and Inundations (NIHYCRI), climate change risk 
management and climate change adaptation practices. The NIHYCRI -related training builds capacity on the 
installation, operation and maintenance of climate change adaptation technologies, the implementation of early 
warning action protocols and operational procedures for long -term monit oring. DEFIS+ will ensure the application 
of these standards to small - and large -scale  water infrastructure through capacity building to be financed by the 
private sector and the Government and provided by specialised technical institutes in Madagascar. Special efforts 
will be made to strengthen women’s participation in WUAs and their repre sentation in leadership positions (with 
a quota of at least 30%) and the development of water resource preservation and management plans.  
Activity 1.1.4. Construction of climate smart water tanks for drip -irrigation and for small livestock.  
This climate adaptation technique will consist of building a surface smart climate water storage system to irrigate 
the terraces and reduce runoff, along with the strengthening of design offices and construction companies in 
terms of NIHYCRI, the protectio n of the irrigated perimeters and the development of anti -erosion devices on the 
watersheds.


---
*Page 34*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 30 
 Climate smart water tanks can either be conventional water storage tanks (gravity water tanks) or buried sand -
filled water tanks (REEP s)58. The storage capacity of the tanks is estimated between 50m3 and 250m3 or even 
more, depending on the availability of water resources (rainfall). Other than REEP s, there is also the ISAA or 
Giant Impluvium for gravity collection and storage of large amounts of rainwater for agriculture, which co nsists of 
a rainwater harvesting system via road gutters to be connected via runoff channels dug in hillside and transport 
them to large reservoirs (hill reservoirs) sealed and equipped with a drainage system to distribute water to crops, 
animals, and/or villages. The two rainwater harvesting techniques represent a modern climate  change  adaptation 
tool, saving time and labou r often associated with water collection especially by women. It is labour intensive and 
necessitates tools and techniques that are mastered by Malagasy farmers and materials that are easily available 
in Madagascar, without the need for importing material from abroad, drilling, pumping, electricity generators or 
sophisticated engineering equipment. This technique has a very limited climate and environmental footprint. This 
activity will include WASH trainings on hand washing and hygiene targeting households . 
The expected results of Output 1.1 are: (i) conducting comprehensive  water cycle management analyses; (ii)  
improved climate resilience of 15 000 hectares of irrigated schemes; (iii) anti -erosion measures are implemented 
on 8 100 hectares of sub -watersheds that drain into irrigated perimeters, of which 5 500 hectares of landscape 
are restored  by women  and youth , and 900 technicians are trained; (iv) 1 400 WUA members and technicians 
are trained on climate change adaptation, water resource preservation and management plans; (vi) 150 units of 
climate smart water tanks  are installed; and (vi) 268 321 farmers, including women and young people, are 
engaged in the effective management of sub –watersheds and irrigated perimeters.  
Output 1.2: Enhanced agro -climatic information systems , new technologies and initiatives  
The activities planned under this Output are the following:  
Activity 1.2.1. Installation of automated agro -meteorological stations and development of flood and drought 
monitoring and forecasting system  
DEFIS+ will support the upgrading of agrometeorological information systems by strengthening the network for 
collecting and processing agro -meteorological data in target areas, which is part of the national network. The 
project will support the collection of accurate climate information (temperature, precipitation, wind, humidity, etc.) 
and promote the expansion of the existing national network through the acquisition and installation of automated 
agrometeorological stations. The stations will be located in  the DEFIS+ project area to fill the gap in the existing 
network of stations. The national hydromet agency will be the implementing partner and will be responsible for 
the operation and maintenance of the stations. Comprehensive collection of meteorologica l data and climate 
information and its correct interpretation will ensure that detailed weather, climate, and agricultural hydrological 
forecasts will support precise short - and long -term climate projections, predict extreme climate events long in 
advance,  manage water resources, crop modelling and seasonal planning in a timely manner to help smallholder 
farmers plan. Satellite data will be assessed together with hydromet variables, drought indices and climate risk 
indices for a drought early warning system  including the use of 1 - to 10 -day flood forecasting for better accuracy.  
The Directorate General of Meteorology already provides  some climate services and makes use of different 
platforms to disseminate information , but many of them are out -of-date.  These services can be further upgraded 
and enhanced with the help of the project. A state -of-the-art flood and drought monitoring system will be 
developed for focused and tailored climate services for hydrological hazards. The system will be driven using 
precipitation from the scientifically valida ted Multi -Source Weighted -Ensemble P recipitation (MSWEP) dataset, 
which merges station s, statistical model s, and satellite information using a sophisticated algorithm. Based on the 
system, the following products will be promoted:  
● Customised rainfall and drought information on a daily/seasonal/longer term basis linked to early warning 
systems for management actions and decision making based on the climate update and monthly climate summary 
is sent to all agriculture and livestock personnel by email;  
● An agricultural newsletter is published quarterly and includes information on seasonal weather outlooks 
(accessible online);  
 
58 REEPS is a technology combining the collection, storage and mobilisation of water that the ENERGIS FD Company developed and p atented 
in 2011 to solve the problems of drying up and evaporation of water collected in the water table and on the surface. It is  based on a biological 
retro filtration in the surface and is a system for storing and reusing rainwater at a lower cost while rigorously respecting  the ecological aspect.

 ● Special brochures (visual illustrations) are published (e.g., on mulching techniques, agroforestry, etc.) 
that are specifically designed to help farmers in times of climatic stress ( e.g., drought) with translation to local 
languages ; 
● An ICT system will be established and will operate for the development of new advisories and warnings 
for agriculture and water management and their dissemination through the media, including SMS and radio.  
● DEFIS+ will offer a diverse range of options for accessing the information to accommodate the specific 
needs of women, and for this reason, a gender needs assessment will be carried out with the aim of providing 
gender -specific information.  
Activity 1.2.2. Capacity building of decentralised government (SRM & DRAEP)  
The project  will also provide support at the regional level to the staff of the Regional Meteorological Services 
(SRM) and Regional Directorates for Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries (DRAEP) through capacity development 
for the analysis, communication and dissemina tion of agrometeorological and climate information. National 
institutions and relevant civil society organisations will be trained and mobilised to better inform farmers and their 
rural organisations and build their capacity for the integration  of climate risk into their decision -making processes 
and good farming practices. In these trainings, specific attention will be given to the gendered dimension of access 
and preferences regarding climate information.  
Training will be provided to SRM staff for data collection and information generation to support the use of new 
technologies such as the flood and drought monitoring system, GIS, and the development of new products, 
including reduced and additional timesca les for weather forecasting to produce information adapted to end users.  
Popularization of climate information will be through local agents, agents of the Regional Directorate of 
Agriculture, technicians, FFS, agents of the SRM and the use of mass media, local radio and fairs. Farmers will 
have access to localised and timely we ather services and long -term weather forecasts and will receive training 
on how to manage weather risks and better plan their farming and livelihood activities.  
To overcome obstacles to local livelihood planning, the project will also establish new partnerships to strengthen 
the producer -user interface, including engaging with NGOs to establish sustainable and cost -effective service 
transfer modes. This will also build user -level capacity for the interpretation of climate data and advice.  
Activity 1.2.3. Promotion and dissemination of Meteorological and Climate Smart Agriculture practices  
DEFIS+ will promote the dissemination of meteorological and CSA information to strengthen the climate resilience 
of dryland farming systems and rainfed upland farming systems (with respect to irrigated areas) through the 
promotion of climate resilient agricultural production, including afforestation, mixed cropping systems and 
improved technologies such as adapted varieties, planting materials and water harvesting.   
DEFIS+ will rely on the local network of seed multiplier farmers and seed producing sites. The multiplication and 
dissemination of locally climate adapted seeds and planting materials will be carried out using community -based 
participatory approaches  (e.g., FFS) , integrated with meteorological information and disseminated among the 
farmers and stakeholders.  Learners at the FFS level will not only be supported in resilient agricultural techniques 
but will also be provided with seeds for the first production season. The seed production centres exist in the 
DEFIS+ regions, but their skills will be improved as will the materials and equipment for the production of basic 
seeds. Thus, DEFIS+ will support the Agnarafaly Seed Production Center (CPSA) and Kianjavato Center in the 
multiplication and promotion of certified or quality declared seeds.  
In addition, agroforestry and landscape management systems will be promoted for the improvement of micro -soil 
bunds, trenches and pits to prevent erosion, as well as several agroforest ry and landscape operations at the local 
level. Water pumping will be done using solar pumps to ensure reduced diesel and gasoline use (full scale up 
and nationwide adoption). The summary of the CSA activities and locations is available in Table 5.2.  
Activity 1.2.4. Promotion and dissemination of sustainable home technologies and social initiatives  
DEFIS+ will promote low -cost on - and off -farm modern  technologies, such as improved cooking stoves and 
handmade briquettes, by using local materials, thus reducing deforestation . Other technologies include solar 
pumps for irrigation, biogas production along with livestock systems, solar milling and dryers for product 
processing. These technologies will contribute to improving efficiency and production while also reducing


---
*Page 36*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 32 
 women’s time burden and workload (thereby impacting their health and nutrition status and indirectly those of 
their young children) . DEFIS+ will promote these technologies and invest in the training of trainers  for the use of 
the technologies and youth associations and groups for the maintenance of the technologies . The scale of the 
technologies will be assessed through a feasibility study and an assessment of the adoption of the technologies 
as well as their supply chains. The technologies will be both hou sehold/farm level and multi -household/farm level  
and will be procured through the DEFIS+ procurement team and local municipalities .  
Some of these tools and technologies (e.g.,  cooking stoves) will be provided to vulnerable households for 
demonstration  and catalytic purposes i.e., generating demand . In IFAD’s experience working with the most 
vulnerable and poor populations in rural areas, transfer of goods is necessary in situations where beneficiaries 
do not have sufficient wealth and liquidity to purchase productive assets. The free distribution targeted at the most 
vulnerable households is  important to enable them to improve  their production systems  and also demonstrate the 
effectiveness of the technologies to promote adoption by other community members who can then invest in the 
technologies . Doing so, they will be able to emerge from poverty and perhaps support other businesses and move 
other people out of poverty. Without external support, th e change in their economic conditions  is less likely . Well 
targeted support to the most vulnerable households will not  distort markets because these individuals, at the 
bottom of the wealth pyramid, are not able to access markets with their own resources today. The project is 
expected to contribute to move them out of poverty so that they eventually become bu yers themselves and 
consequently stimulate the growth of the market.  DEFIS+ will also engage youth groups and associations to 
participate as entrepreneurs in the supply chain of the technologies once the demand is generated working 
through other IFAD financed projects such as FORMAPROD/PROGRES.  
The expected results of Output 1.2 are: (i) six automated agro -meteorological stations are installed and 
operational; (ii) a flood and drought monitoring and forecasting system is developed and operationalized; (iii) 24 
DRAEP and SRM teams are trained and involved in processing and disseminating agro -meteorological data; (iv) 
178 881 farmers  will use agro -meteorological  and CSA  information during  the agricultural production seasons ;  
(v) 268 321 farmers use low-cost and modern  technologies; ( vi) support is provided to  CPSA for the production 
of basic seeds of stress -tolerant varieties ; (vii) a t least three new methods of integrated pest management 
techniques adapted to the context of smallholder farms in the project area are promoted and  resilient fodder 
production is integrated on 1 500 ha; and (viii) CSA, afforestation and agroforestry approaches are implemented 
on 10  000 hectares of land.  
Outcome 2: Increased income generation through improved market accessibility  
In Madagascar, floods and road submersion by runoff are frequent, especially in the DEFIS+ area. Besides, most 
of the communal roads are unpaved, so they are practically impassable  in the rainy season. There are also many 
crossings given the important hydrographic network of the area, but their condition often handicaps the 
accessibility of the villages and the outlet s for production. This makes it difficult to get products to market. Extreme 
climatic events, such as floods and cyclones, in combination with  poor quality and conventionally designed roads, 
often cause erosion and flooding in the surrounding environment or the road embankments , which result in losses 
of agricultural land and products.  DEFIS+ will, therefore, invest in: (i) harvesting water for agricultural purposes 
using rural roads while improving their resilience; and (ii) implementation of a wide variety of GR 4W techniques 
to secure the transport function of roads year-round , protect the environment and agricultural activities around 
rural ro ads. 
According to Madagascar’s  NDC and the National Adaptation Plan, the adoption of climate resilient infrastructure 
is among the sectoral priorities for adapting to climate change. In fact, to link small producers to the market, 
DEFIS+ will strengthen the climate resilience of rural roads, in particular with regard to rainwater, which is the 
main factor of damage and the main enemy of rural roads infrastructure. Other support and technology transfer 
already implemented in other countries under the GR4W approach will be useful for the implementation of the 
climate resilience -building activities for rural road infrastructure under DEFIS+. Also in this case, there might be 
some unexpected negative externalities resulting from road upgrading, such as GHG emissions although in the 
case of Madagascar given the low levels of road infrastructure development the o verall impact will be low.


---
*Page 37*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 33 
 The G R4W techniques that will be used in the DEFIS+ project will be drawn from the recently published World 
Bank Guideline on GR 4W59,60, which documents a wide variety of strategies and techniques on how to use roads 
as instruments for beneficial water management and climate resilience in different geographies. According to the 
GR4W Guideline s, the opportunities are abundant,  and an extensive  analysis is available in the infrastructure 
feasibility study (Annex 2c).   
The rehabilitation of rural roads will improve access to markets, a priority identified in Madagascar's national 
development plan. It will lead to an increase in traffic and therefore an increase in GHG emissions. However, this 
increase in emissions, which  is linked to the implementation of the National Development Plan and efforts to 
increase the resilience of the most vulnerable population, is considered marginal. It will not contribute significantly 
to national GHG emissions and does not contradict the c ountry's NDC or the Paris Agreement, which recognises 
that the fight against climate change is always linked to national development and food security. Madagascar 
accounts for just over 0.10% of global GHG emissions.  
Output 2.1: Reinforced rural access roads  
To minimise damage, water will be systematically directed to the land rather than running along the road surface. 
To direct water to the land, different design parameters will be considered, such as regular slope reversal, 
avoidance of sunken roads and inclusion of basic drainage. In addit ion, a wide variety of techniques will be used 
to preserve unpaved roads, enable water harvesting and reduce erosion including water bars, rolling dips, 
infiltration bunds and vegetative measures among others.  More information is available in Annex 2c.  
The activities planned under this Output are the following:  
Activity 2.1.1. Reinforcement of unpaved rural access roads to reduce climate change impacts and water 
collection along these roadways for agricultural use  
In the context of DEFIS+, the following GR 4W techniques will be explored while rehabilitation works are performed 
through structured labour -intensive work:  
● Using road -surface cross drainage systems: a series of rolling dips (small depressions with a small bump) 
or water bars (small, slanted humps) to divert water from the road surface to the land for productive use. These 
structures have two purposes: (i) pre vent water from flowing beside the road surface by diverting it from the road, 
(ii) through the diversions water is conveyed to an area where it can be  beneficially  deployed, e.g., recharge of 
groundwater, feeding a spring or directly flowing into an on-farm pond to be used for irrigation.  
● Using infiltration bunds to slow down the side erosion from the roads and promote recharge of aquifers. 
This is a typical issue on many unpaved roads where road runoff builds up, causing erosion and sedimentation. 
It is best combined with vegetative protec tion to reduce the erosive effect of runoff from the road template, in 
particular if no side drain is provided and the road template is out sloped  or crowned.  
● Monitoring groundwater recharge percentages will employ borehole dippers, also known as Water Level 
Dip Meters. DEFIS+ will use Water Level Dip Meters to determine the water level within a borehole, piezometer 
pipe or sump .61 It should be noted that the potential concomitant impacts, such as extension of flood duration and 
reduction of peak discharge with the proposed structures for underground water systems, are well captured in 
the DEFIS+ ESMP’s section related to the constr uction of water tanks –  including measures of mitigation and 
indicators of achievement/sources of verification.   
● Complementing a range of bioengineering methods to revitalise vegetation in the road adjacency. 
Bioengineering is the use of plants for slope stabilisation and runoff control. It involves using plant parts such as 
roots, cuttings and stems as a cost -effect ive and locally adaptable means of erosion control. It ranges from 
planting deep -rooted species to a combination of vegetation with civil engineering structures. It helps not only to 
preserve the slopes, in addition, it buffers the water through higher inf iltration capacity.  
● Routine maintenance, periodic maintenance and rehabilitation/reconstruction works will need to be 
systematised and applied along the construction and rehabilitation of unpaved rural roads. DEFIS+ will give 
special attention to ensuring  that women are involved in maintenance and rehabilitation/reconstruction works . 
 
59 Van Steenbergen, F., Arroyo -Arroyo, F., Rao, K., Hulluka, T. A., Woldearegay, K., & Deligianni, A. (2021). Green Roads for Water: 
Guidelines for Road Infrastructure in Support of Water Management and Climate Resilience.

The expected results  of Output 2.1 are: 540 km of unpaved rural access roads are re habilitated  or improved 
with climate -resilient planning, including cross -drainage systems, and the project contributes to recharging 
aquifers. This also includes maintenance services for the entire duration of the project.  
 
For Activity 2.1.1, IFAD as AE will ensure the EE conducts, prior to undertaking any construction works, 
comprehensive technical assessments and feasibility studies for each of the sub -projects identified and this shall 
be done prior to launching of the design and constructio n works. The designs to be implemented for each of the 
sub-projects shall be informed by the output and results from these feasibility studies.   
 
The feasibility studies to be conducted encompass analysing  the baseline situation, identifying location -specific 
climate risks and devising appropriate measures for climate proofing the infrastructure under consideration 
against the identified climate hazards and integrating the climate resilience measure into th e designs. The 
methodology involves a systematic assessment utilizing data, local knowledge, stakeholder engagement, and the 
development of the appropriate adaptation approach. The expected deliverables include a detailed technical and 
feasibility report, climate -proofing design approaches, and the recommendations for integrating the resilience 
measures into the construction to be submitted to the GCF’.  This also applies to Activities 1.1.1, 1.1.2 and 1.1.4.    
 
 
IFAD , in collaboration with the EE , has clearly defined criteria that were used in the selection/prioritization of the 
roads and irrigation schemes to be rehabilitated/retrofitted under this project. Hence, if a particular sub -project 
that has been earmarked and listed in the FP could no l onger be implemented due to changes in the site condition 
during implementation, an eligible replacement would be identified by applying the same criteria.  The selection 
of sub -projects is based on the identification and prioritization of projects using specific criteria aimed at 
maximizing their impact on adaptation and resilience building in the regions in Madagascar considered.  
 
Output 2.2: Improved capacity to build and maintain rural infrastructure  
 
DEFIS+ will equip technical staff with the basic design, construction, operation and maintenance of Green Roads, 
well-tuned to the local conditions, so as to make rural roads and the surrounding environments more resilient to 
climate change.  
The activities planned under this Output are the following:  
Activity 2.2.1. Training on construction standards related to climate change  
The Malagasy Standards for the Construction of Road Infrastructure Resistant to Floods and Geological 
Phenomena, developed in 2020, are based on the Guide for Road Protection Against Floods in Madagascar 
(GPRCIM). The Standards will be part of the training  that will be provided to key municipal staff as well as several 
other construction areas. Thus, they will be trained on the operational procedures for the long -term maintenance 
and the scaling of the technologies of climate -resilient roads and of water co llection according to the operation 
and maintenance plans and road construction standards. The training will include the design and implementation 
of a long -term monitoring framework for road -specific climate proofing measures.  
In accordance with the categories of personnel working in the municipalities, one recommendation would consist 
of designating the Mayor as the first person in charge in matters of Authority capable of enforcing these standards, 
s/he must be assisted direct ly by the technicians of the Town hall who would be trained in these standards and 
will have to ensure the relay between the municipality and the road contractor . 
The expected result of Output 2.2 is: 178 881 regional and local technicians are trained on road construction 
standards related to climate change, such as the Flood Protection Standards. Half of them are women and 
vulnerable people.  
Outcome 3: Improved food and nutrition security  
To promote greater uptake and ensure that food and nutrition security will be long -lasting, knowledge products 
on best practices will be elaborated and disseminated under DEFIS+.

 Output 3.1: Knowledge on climate change and food security  generated and shared  
The activities planned under this Output are the following:  
Activity 3.1.1. Training rural development actors on climate change and food production systems for enhanced 
nutrition  
Training smallholder farmers will build their capacities, especially those of women, to improve agricultural 
resilience and productivity by effectively identifying their needs, managing rainwater and soil moisture and 
reducing or potentially eliminating wa ter stress during periods of short -term  drought. The innovative aspects of 
DEFIS+ are linked to the adopted methodology. The FFS methodologies will create a tiered training system that 
will draw on farmer experience and institutional agronomic science to e nsure the adoption of climate smart farming 
practices. Training of trainers and farmer -to-farmer training will ensure peer support, exchange of experiences 
based on on -farm practices (for example, conservation agriculture and drip -irrigation) and increased  institutional 
experience and capacities for agricultural extension.  Rain-fed farmers will be able to manage soil, water and 
biomass in order to maximise water availability through on -site or off -site water collection and improve water 
productivity through  the use of water -saving technologies. To complement the training on climate change, training 
on cross -cutting topics, such as nutrition and gender empowerment in the context of climate change will be 
organised and integrated  into the FFS.  
DEFIS+ will support each FFS (approximately 25 people per training course) through: (i) the installation of a plot 
of 0.5 ha, made available by a producer/member of a CBO or community within the development pole (with priority 
given to women); and (ii) the  application of technical itineraries and new practices at the level of individual farmer ’s 
plots. The main beneficiaries of the FFS will primarily be young people (male and female) who will use their plots 
for training, facilitating demonstrations, exchan ges and reporting results. The FFS organisation must therefore be 
flexible to allow women to participate actively.  DEFIS+ will use the FFS structures already established under 
DEFIS and integrate the climate change resilience building practices and technologies.    
The FFS approach is intended to support the strengthening of farmer  knowledge through field observations.  The 
FFS approach is an effective peer to peer learning  system that enables farmers to identify challenges they face 
in their agricultural production activities and identify solutions in a collaborative facilitated manner.  Among other 
things, the project will prepare learning materials for the FFS, train farmers in climate change adaptation practices 
for sustainable production, provide tools and training  to enable them to adopt more efficient soil, water and input 
management practices, and train farmers in agricultural techniques such as the Sustainable Intensive/Improved 
Rice Cultivation ( SRI/SRA ) systems.  
Activity 3.1.2. Exchange visits on climate resilient food systems  
To promote the exchange of knowledge and best practices related to climate resilient farming practices, DEFIS+ 
will support exchange visits and study tours at the community and local levels. Specific measures will be taken to 
ensure the involvement of wome n in these exchange visits ( e.g., using a participant quota of at least 50%; 
organising women -only exchange visits and planning visits to successful female producers).  
The participants will be identified in a participatory, transparent, and consensual process involving the beneficiary 
communities, representatives of local and regional farmer organizations, strategic partners (Government and 
State representatives) and DEF IS+’s team. Eligibility criteria based, on age, gender, degree of vulnerability, level 
of schooling, socio -economic status, etc., will be defined. Thus, the approach will ensure that target groups, 
especially the most vulnerable, are the beneficiaries of D EFIS+ support.  
 
Activity 3.1.3. Capitalization of best practices and knowledge on food and nutrition security measures  
Local skills and knowledge will be leveraged, for instance, through the development of construction operations 
manuals, in order to scale up innovations. DEFIS+ will also support knowledge capturing studies on the climate 
adaptation and mitigation options and strategies developed by smallholder farmers,  by developing and proposing 
the design and composition of a package of materials, and communication formats , e.g., Documentary Clip 
Abécédaire game, educational games, radio broadcasts, technical data sheet and vo ice mess ages. These 
knowledge products will pay special attention to gender dimensions  for access and use .

 As part of this capitalization process, DEFIS+ will consolidate the efforts made by programs financed by the World 
Bank to enrich and improve the database on LAUREL (Land use planning for enhanced resilience of landscape) 
for assessing, simulating, evaluat ing and re -orienting land use and land use change processes and to develop a 
land degradation baseline and a Prototype Land Use Change Simulation Platform (LANDSIM -P). The platform is 
essential for evaluating changes in land use  and forms of degradation or  restoration in order to make decisions 
on the orientations to be taken on the watershed within the framework of territorial planning.  
It should be noted that DEFIS+ has already arranged and discussed the specific requirements and procedures 
for obtaining licenses, permits, and authorizations for access to private/public land with all stakeholders and will 
provide, on a case -by-case basis , the necessary licenses, authorizations and permits. For example, in the case 
of private property, access to land rights requires either a land title issued by the Ministry of Land Management 
and Land Services (MATSF), or a land certificate issued by dece ntralized authorities (communes) in areas where 
there are Land Offices. This issue and all its details are extensively discussed in Annex 9.  
The expected results of Output 3.1 are: (i) 447 20 1 farmers are trained on farming practices adapted to climate 
change, mitigation, use of stress -tolerant seed varieties and materials through FFS, as well as nutrition (including 
healthy diets) and gender; (ii) 30 exchange visits are held to disseminate goo d practices on climate change 
adaptation; (iii) land degradation baseline is enriched (updated with assessment of current levels of degradation 
and regional specificity); and (iv) at least 20 thematic know ledge products are published on best practices for 
climate change adaptation.

Table 5.1 summarizes the adaptation measures for important food crops by area and priority. Some of the 
interventions such as the agro -forestry and improved soil and water conservation measures are no -regret climate 
change adaptation measures that are accessible to and feasible for the farmers to adopt. Furthermore , an 
extensive discussion is available in Annex 2b  on the various climate change adaptation options . Table 5.2 
synthesises the CSA benefits for the DEFIS+ regions.  
DEFIS+ capitalizes on the lessons learned from past IFAD projects. A summary of such lessons is included below.  
● Drip irrigation technology is emerging as a suitable solution in Madagascar dry regions.  
● The innovative underground water collection technology (REEP) has proven to be very relevant in the 
current context of drought, helping to provide access to water. It will be further developed within the framework of 
DEFIS+ for the collection of surface water at the level of road  networks . 
● The use of improved rice cropping techniques (SRI/SRA) has proven its success with a remarkable 
increase in yield and rice productivity in its intervention zones and will be further developed by DEFIS+.  
● The implementation of agroecological blocks in the DEFIS regions (not the DEFIS+ regions) has 
confirmed the effectiveness of this technique in terms of climate change resilience. The first perceived changes 
concern a better control of the agricultural calendar, the use of quality seeds, a better knowledge of the optimal 
doses of fertilisers (particularly organic fertilisers), better water management, and the adoption of better pest  
control techniques. The blocks will be further developed in DEFIS+ regions, taking into account the specific agro -
ecological characteristics of each area.  
● IFAD ha s trained champion farmers and livestock breeders from the FFSs who are able to disseminate 
their skills to other producers. The training sessions for trainers in the FFS and the association of producers into 
POs have helped to strengthen social cohesion.  
● IFAD projects  have included a module on climate change in the Gender Action Learning System (GALS) 
(a community -led empowerment methodology that uses principles of inclusion to improve income, and the food 
and nutrition security of vulnerable people in a gender -equitable wa y), which besides improving gender relations 
is also allowing for the adoption of good practices in adapting to climate change and preserving the environment. 
The methodology works with households (both females and males) to influence shifts towards positive gender 
relations and social norms and behaviours.  
 
B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)  
The Executing Entity is the Republic of Madagascar (RoM) through its Ministry of Economy and Finance (MEF) 
and Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MINAE). The GCF funds will be received by MEF and the Funded 
activities will be implemented by MINAE.  A legally binding Financing Agreement (Subsidiary Agreement) , which 
will be signed by MEF  shall detail the  specific roles and responsibilities of the relevant governmental ministries, 
MEF and MINAE, involved in the implementation of the Project. The Subsidiary Agreement shall establish the 
terms for the financing and implementation of Outcomes, Outputs and Act ivities and include the relevant 
obligations of the Executing Entity in respect of the implementation of the overall Funded Activity, including 
compliance with the eligibility criteria (as set out in the “Eligibility Criteria”) and the Project Implementati on Manual. 
The Executing Entity through MINAE will select the beneficiaries of all Activities of the Project (the “Final 
Beneficiaries”) and procure service providers for specific Project Activities in accordance with the Eligibility Criteria 
and the PIM. The Executing Entity through the MINAE will enter into contracts for procured services with the 
selected service  providers.  
From a legal point of view, MEF will sign the Financing Agreement as the government's representative, but there 
are cases where it is MINAE that sign s the FA as the governor of IFAD. In the case  of DEFIS+ , MEF will represent 
the Government of Madagascar in accordance with Decree 2019 -093 setting the attributions of the Ministry and 
its general organization. MEF ensures the planning, coordination and monitoring -evaluation of the country's 
medium - and long -term development programs and projects at all levels and the coordination of cooperation at 
the national level. MEF also manages, controls,  and evaluates external funds and contributes to the harmonization


---
*Page 44*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 40 
 of cooperation with donors. Both MEF and MINAE are fully committed to DEFIS+ and ensuring its successful 
implementation . 
The DEFIS+ activities to be funded by the GCF (DEFIS+) will be fully integrated62 into the DEFIS program, which 
is a large agricultural program of the Malagasy Government that has been placed under the technical supervision 
of MINAE.  MINAE has been working with IFAD for 40 years and houses a permanent unit that oversees program 
and project implementation (CAPFIDA). Owing to an implementation arrangement that rests on country systems 
at both national and decentralised levels, DEFIS+ int erventions will be in synergy with the on -going and planned 
investments of all technical and financial partners in the areas of climate change, nutrition and gender.  
Management, coordination and supervision of DEFIS+ activities  
National Steering Committee  
A National Steering Committee (NSC) will oversee the activities of DEFIS+ at the national level. The Secretary -
General of MINAE will chair the NSC, which will comp rise the following members: (i) the General Project 
Coordinator of MINAE; (ii) a Director in the Ministry in charge of Finance; (iii) a Director in the Ministry in charge 
of regional planning; (iv) a Director in the Ministry in charge of the Environment; (v)  the National Climate Change 
Officer; (vi) a Director in the Ministry of Public Works; (vii) a representative Director in the Ministry in charge Social 
Protection and Gender; (viii) The President and three senior representatives of the Chamber of Agricultu re; and 
(ix) the President and one senior representative of the Federation of Chambers of Commerce and Industry. The 
secretariat will be provided by the Coordinator of the PCU. The NSC will meet twice a year and its main role is to: 
(i) validate DEFIS+ ann ual work plans and budget (AWP/Bs); (ii) evaluate progress made on project activities, 
their results and their alignment with strategies for rural areas and (iii) manage project -related grievances that 
cannot be resolved at the regional level.  
Regional Steering and Monitoring Committee  
Regional oversight will be provided by the Regional Steering and Monitoring Committees (RSMCs). The members 
of the RSMCs are: the Head of the Region; the Directorate of Rural Development; Directors of the Regional 
Directorate of Agriculture and Livestock a nd Fisheries and the Inter regional Directorate for the Environment and 
Sustainable Development (DIREDD); representatives of the Regional Steering and Allocation Committee; the 
Assembly of Regional Agriculture (RACs); and Regional Farmer  Organisations . The Coordinator  at the regional 
level will assume the secretariat of the RSMC.  The RSMC will meet twice a year and its main role is to: (i) review 
DEFIS+ annual work plans and budget (AWP/Bs); (ii) assess progress made on project activities and their results 
and (iii) manage project -related grievances.  
Programme Coordination Unit  
The Programme Coordination Unit (PCU) will coordinate and manage DEFIS+ resources and activities in 
accordance with the financing agreement, project manuals (operations, financial management and administration 
procedures , monitoring and evaluation) and AWP/Bs, under the supervision of the RSMC, the NSC and IFAD.  
The PCU will be responsible for: (i) the management of the project’s human, financial and physical resources; (ii) 
the preparation of AWP/Bs and project procurement plans; (iii) the coordination of the implementation of the 
activities; (iv) the monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of the project’s activities and results; (v) the coordination and 
monitoring of the work/activities of partners and service providers; (vii) reporting and submitting activity and 
financial reports to MEF, MINAE and IFAD on a regular ba sis (minimum twice a year in the case of IFAD, so as 
to fulfil IFAD’s semi -annual reporting obligation vis -à-vis GCF); (viii) liaising regularly with regional government 
authorities and decentralised services and other actors in the DEFIS+ intervention zon es; (ix) ensuring the 
compliance of IFAD’s safeguards during project implementation activities (i.e. ESMP, Gender Action Plan).  
Planning and coordination of activities at the regional level will be provided by two inter -regional coordination units 
located in: (i) Fianarantsoa for the Ihorombe, Haute Matsiatra and Amoro n’'i Mania regions; (ii) Manakara for the 
Vatovavy, Fitovinany and Atsimo Atsinanana regions.  
A participatory approach will be used to identify and prioritise rural roads. Once identified , the feasibility studies 
and technical designs will be undertaken during the first and second year of project implementation. The feasibility 
studies will include environment and social impact assessments  and the development of site-specific  risk 
management plans. The works on the roads will be undertaken during years two, three and four of project 
implementation to allow for the evaluation of use and benefits in the last two years of project implementation. The 
sequencing of studies and works will also apply to the irrigation infrastructure to be rehabilitated. The execution 
will include beneficiaries’ contributions through materials and labour, control and superv ision by the enterprise 
 
62 The full integration refers to activities being incorporated (timing, sequencing, approach) in the implementation to ensure t he results and 
overall objectives outlined in DEFIS are achieved in a cost effective and efficient manner.

 and by the rural engineering department. Further details on the sequencing of the studies and works in the 
different regions are included in Annex 2c.  
For the activities related to the low emission agricultural production system, the implementation approach will 
start with the identification of sites based on a needs assessment , beneficiary awareness raising and technical 
feasibility. Structuring and mobilisation of in-kind contributions from beneficiaries  (5%) will be developed to 
enhance ownership and sustainability of the adopted  measures . Lessons learned from previous IFAD projects: 
use of drip technology, underground water collection technology, surface water collection at road level for 
agricultural use, agro -ecological blocks, will be capitalised.  
To strengthen  knowledge generation o n climate change, the implementation  approach will consider the profiling 
of the target group (location, roles,  and importance in economic life). An analysis of the socio -economic profile of 
the target group (women, youth, illiterate) and an inventory of training needs and capacity building in the sites will 
be conducted to ensure an alignment between needs and knowledge . Training streams/themes will be validated 
and prioriti sed, followed by the development of training plans by theme  and by target group category. Forms of 
post-training support will be identified . 
For the implementation of the activities, the PCU will adhere to the Project Implementation Manual that will be 
elaborated detailing the approach. On the ground implementation will entail establishment of  operational 
partnerships with state institutions and service contracts with private operators and NGOs awarded through 
competitive processes. For both private and public sector operators, the condition for the continuation of their 
collaboration on the pr oject is the delivery of the annual results defined in the conventions/contracts/protocols. 
Identified partners included government institutions such as the National Office of Nutrition and Regional Offices 
of Nutrition, and NGOs such as FAFAFI, CTAS, AFAFI, CFAMMA, FOFIFA, FIFAMANOR and private sector 
players such as STOI , Société 3S, SSM, OM Kompiva, agrival LFL, Tozzi Green, MAC SIOU KING Alixia 
(ENTREPRISE PLUS), Sté 3S Union MAMIBE and La Myriade.  
DEFIS+ climate change adaptation activities will be implemented to complement the interventions of other 
partners, namely the GIZ -supported PRADA project; AfDB’s PAPAM project; the CI/EIB Sustainable Landscapes 
in Eastern Madagascar Project, among others. The project will also take into account initiatives for which other 
agencies plan to submit funding proposals to the GCF.  
To support the PCU team ( Figure 18 ) with the implementation of climate change adaptation activities, six 
personnel will be recruited through a competitive process carried out in accordance with the modalities in effect 
for IFAD -supported projects. The positions are: one climate change adaptation specialist; one finance officer; one 
M&E officer for the climate change adaptation activities; one M&E assistant for knowledge, GIS and database 
management; one social and environmental safeguards expert who will also be responsible for the environmental 
and social management plans (ESMPs) and one gender expert, both also involved in M&E procedures; the latter 
four will be integrated in the DEFIS M&E unit; all personnel will be hired on one -year contracts that may be 
renewed after a performance assessment has been done. The CAPFIDA will also  support the PCU by providing 
advice and expertise on specific aspects, including monitoring and evaluation, knowledge management and 
financial management.       
National Designated Authority (NDA)  
Through its responsibilities, the National Designated Authority (NDA) of the Green Climate Fund of Madagascar 
will contribute to the monitoring and evaluation of the project throughout its cycle. Thus, it will monitor the 
implementation of project activitie s in the field and assess project indicators including the reduction of greenhouse 
gas (GHG) emissions and the assessment of the degree of communities’ vulnerability.  Thus, the NDA’s 
Adaptation Unit (NDA -AU) will have an active role in the national adaptation planning p rocesses and its 
contribution will be incorporated into relevant DEFIS+ activities.  The monitoring will be based on secondary data 
produced by recurrent statistical surveys and framework surveys carried out at the national level and on the 
results of studies and surveys allowing for measuring  the changes brought about by the project at the target level. 
Results including progress on the Gender Action plan will be assessed during supervision missions. The impact 
of the activities on GHG emissions will be analysed using the EX -ACT tool. Adaptation activities will be analysed  
and monitored in consultation with the NDA -AU. Further details are available in Annex 11 on Monitoring and 
Evaluation.  
Joint supervision of the program will be provided by the Government of Madagascar (MINAE, MEF, MEDD DNA; 
NDA, and ONE) and IFAD. Supervision missions will be carried out twice a year and their main goals will be: (i) 
to assess progress on the implementatio n of each of DEFIS +  components; (ii) evaluate progress on the expected 
outcomes and outputs for each of the project’s components in comparison to what had been established in the 
concept note or other project documents; (iii) assess the quality of targeti ng and gender equality; (iv) analyse the 
different fiduciary aspects of the project (funding allocations, payment and disbursement commitments, conformity

 of expenditures with AWP/Bs and concept note, accounting records) and review outcomes of the external audits

Figure 18: Organisational chart of the DEFIS+ program  
 
Fiduciary aspects  
The financial management and procurement procedures to be used for the GCF funds are the ones specified in 
IFAD ’s Manual of Administrative, Financial and Accounting Procedures, which has been validated by the 
Government of Madagascar and IFAD. The project’s accounting will be done on approved  TOM2PRO software 
and will follow the rules and practices of other IFAD -funded projects in Madagascar, expanding the scope to 
include all reporting dimensions required by GCF to track expenditures. The project will disburse funds according 
to the plan of ac tivities defined in the AWP/B, which includes the disbursement and procurement plans.  
Flow of funds   
The GCF financing will be made available to Madagascar in the form of both grant and loan funds. This will be 
operationalized through:  
● a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA), between GCF and IFAD, ruling the contribution of GCF to IFAD for 
which funds will flow into a dedicated bank account held by IFAD;  
● a subsequent Subsidiary Agreement (SA), between IFAD and Madagascar (i.e. ‘’Financing Agreement’’ 
as per IFAD standard terminology) on which basis the proceeds of the GCF contribution (i) will be provided (on -
lent/on -granted) to Madagascar in the agreed de nomination currency (i.e. USD) and (ii) will flow from IFAD to the 
Borrower (Madagascar -MEF) into a Project Designated Account (DA) in USD opened at the Central Bank of 
Madagascar (CBM) . 
In the FAA, the ‘’GCF’s Financial Terms and Conditions of Grants and Concessional Loans’’ will apply to the GCF 
contribution (grant, loan) to IFAD. The same terms and conditions will be maintained in the SA with regards to 
the GCF funds on -lent/granted by IFAD to Madagascar. It is also relevant to mention that, in the SA, ‘’IFAD 
Lending Terms and Conditions’’ will apply to any IFAD co -financing (with own IFAD resources) to Madagascar.  
Disbursement and payment procedure


---
*Page 47*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 43 
 The administration of the external funds of a public institution, in Madagascar, is regulated by decrees  N°2015 -
1457 and N°2016 -1160 , which stipulate that all funds for projects managed by public institutions must be 
deposited in a designated account (DA) opened at CBM. MEF authorises  the opening of the Project DA, in foreign 
currency ( e.g., USD) at CBM. MEF (the borrower) approves the requests for disbursement of funds into the Project 
DA, which are prepared by the Project and Line Ministry (Lead Project Agency -LPA) on the basis of the supporting 
documentation defined in the Subsidiary Agree ment ( i.e., ‘’Financing Agreement’’ as per IFAD standard 
terminology). Afterwards, funds will be converted from the foreign currency into the national currency upon 
transfers from Project DA to operational accounts opened at commercial banks in Ariary (MGA), the nati onal 
currency. MEF controls the flow of funds to the operational accounts. The opening of operational accounts is 
authorised  by MEF on request of the Project/LPA. The replenishment of operational accounts will be done after 
justification of the utilisation  of a part of the previously allocated amount. The operational accounts will be used 
to make payments, for activity -related expenses, to third parties (Implementing Partners, Contractors, Service 
Providers) on the basis of signed agreements (MoU/Contracts) .  
Counterpart financing  
The Government of Madagascar will take measures to exempt the project’s expenses from taxes and duties, such 
as including annual budgetary provisions for exemptions on taxes and duties charged on products imported by 
the DEFIS+ project. The value -added tax  and import duties and taxes are subject to the rules on the execution of 
public expenditures. The office of the Treasury  of Madagascar will assume the taxes and duties vis -à-vis 
suppliers. The Government will also contribute its own internal resources to the account opened with the Treasury. 
Beneficiaries will also contribute in cash or in kind. The beneficiaries ’ contribution , whether in cash or in kind  will 
be fully tracked during implementation.   
Audit of accounts  
The internal audit will be carried out by MINAE units established for this purpose, which are: the CAPFIDA internal 
controller who reports to project coordinators; and this department’s Management and Internal Audit Control Unit, 
which reports to MINAE Gen eral Secretariat. Furthermore, the project’s accounts will be verified annually through 
external audits conducted by  independent and qualified auditing firms in accordance with IFA D Handbook for 
Financial Reporting and Auditing, and in compliance with the applicable GCF requirements ( i.e., expenditure 
eligibility, categorisation vis -à-vis GCF budget categories, etc.). A certified copy of the auditor’s report will be 
submitted to IFAD a few weeks before IFAD’s deadline for sending such a report to GCF.  
B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
Climate change and the effects of the El Niño/La Niña phenomena are increasing  the frequency and severity of 
extreme weather events such as droughts and floods  and will continue to be major impediments to sustainable 
development. Indeed, climate change and its effects aggravate the vulnerability of populations that are already 
affected by poverty , food and nutrition insecurity. The DEFIS+ activities are targeted at climate change adaptation 
with mitigation co -benefits . In the context of common but differentiated responsibilities and respective capabilities 
and its fair and ambitious NDC, the country has estimated the amount of the adaptation cost to be USD 28.713 
billion. Reiterating the very low responsibility in terms  of GHG emissions the NDC highlights, Madagascar spends 
a substantial part of its annual budget on social services and infrastructure in response to the adverse effects of 
climate change (impacts of floods, droughts and cyclones). The precarious economic s ituation of Madagascar 
entails that the implementation of the NDC is condition al on  the availability of external financial support, especially 
through the financial mechanisms established under the UNFCCC, hence,  this request for GCF resources is 
mainly in the form of a grant (74% while 26% will be a loan)63. The GCF resources will supplement the limited 
national resources, while also ensuring debt sustainability for Madagascar as a country that is highly vulnerable 
to the effects of climate change.  
Due to its history of recurrent political crises and economic stagnation, Madagascar is one of the poorest countries 
in the world with a highly narrow fiscal space for investments in key sectors, that will stimulate inclusive and broad -
based economic growth such as agriculture, social services and basic infrastructure. The degradation of 
ecosystems (land, water and biodiversity) and the narrow base of basic infrastructure have been accentuated by 
the recurrence of extreme events resulting from the effects of climate change. Madagascar ’s Country Strategic 
Opportunities Program (COSOP)64 jointly developed with IFAD highlights  the limited performance of the 
agricultural sector as one of the main causes of rural poverty. This poor performance is mainly due to exposure 
to climate change, low levels of public and private investment, low productivity of  production systems and the 
weak institutional capacities of sector actors. The average yields of most food crops are well below their potential. 
For example, the average annual yield for irrigated rice is estimated at 3.0 tons/ha against a potential of up to 5.0 
 
63 The Economic and Financial Analysis provides further details on the justification for grant and loan resources.   
64 The COSOP outlines IFAD’s engagement in a specified country and is jointly developed with the Government for a time period of  
approximately 5 -6 years.


---
*Page 48*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 44 
 or even 6.0 tons/ha; for maize, 0.9 tons/ha against a potential of 4.0 tons/ha; for beans, 0.8 ton/ha against 2.0 
tons/ha; and for cassava, 7 tons/ha against 30 tons/ha. The agriculture sector is deemed a risky business to 
invest in, due to the uncertainti es inherent to weather, yields, prices, government policies, global markets and 
other factors that can result in fluctuations in farming activities and related income generation. Such market failure 
is exacerbated by climate change and disincentives the pr ivate sector to invest in agriculture, therefore justifying 
the request for GCF financing. There are several barriers that contribute to the suboptimal allocation of goods 
and services in the areas targeted by DEFIS+. Farmers  have limited access to productivity enhancing 
technologies due to their remoteness. Without Farmers’ Organisations, farms located in the southern part of 
Madagascar generate a  limited marketable surplus. With climate change  and the associated extreme events, 
some production areas can remain inaccessible for several months following a flood.      
The positive results of IFA D’s long-standing investments in Madagascar make it clear that the development of 
inclusive agricultural value chains (from inputs, production, post -harvest and marketing) is a powerful vehicle for 
rural transformation and improved food and nutrition security . With the normalisation of the political and social 
situation in the country, DEFIS+ investments will contribute to restoring, reinforcing and securing the productive 
bases of rural households, by connecting them to production and market support services and building their 
resilience to climate change and food and nutrition insecurity. This will contribute significantly to Madagascar’s 
efforts to transform its economy sustainably and adapt to climate change. The objectives and priorities of DEFIS+ 
fit perf ectly within the framework of national policies aimed at transforming the agricultural sector from one that is 
predominantly subsistence -based with low productivity levels and unsustainable practices such as slash and burn 
farming to one that is more resil ient and sustainable,  and which contributes significantly to improving food and 
nutrition security.  
The GCF resources are requested mostly in the form of non -reimbursable grant given the country's situation and 
needs particularly the increasing debt situation and the target beneficiaries being vulnerable populations. The 
outstanding debt contracted or gu aranteed by the State (CGE) stood at 33.8% of GDP at the end of 2017 
compared with 26.0% at the end of 2013.  In Madagascar, a recent analysis conducted by the IMF has highlighted 
that the public debt could reach about 49.0% of GDP in 2025, higher than the 46.0% of GDP in 2020.65 It is thus 
recommended to maximise the use of non -reimbursable grants (Reports on the analysis of debt sustainability of 
Madagascar, Ministry of Finance and Budget Ministry of Economy and Planning June 2018). The Economic and 
Financial Analysis further emp hasises that without such a non -reimbursable grant from the GCF, project activities 
would not be financially viable. The  vulnerability of the target populations is exacerbated by  climate change  and 
the extremely low adaptive capacity at various levels makes it particularly difficult to use  non-grant financial 
instruments. The limited public funding available for the proposed DEFIS+ activities will not allow timely action 
and will result in further degradation of natural resources and increasing food and water insecurity among the 
rural popul ations of southern Madagascar. Attracting private capital for innovative financing is very challenging 
and would require a significant expectation of profit to mitigate the perceived risk, such high profitability would be 
incompatible with the target benef iciaries.  In 2019, the World Bank Doing Business ranked Madagascar the 161st 
country out of 190, and specifically 132nd in terms of access to credit.66 Therefore, without the participation of the 
GCF as a key investor to build the foundation, it would be almost impossible to raise further private funds for 
climate change -related  investment in Madagascar. Furthermore, the downside risk for smallholder farmers 
resulting from cost and sales pressures indicates the need for grant -based support as well as mechanisms to 
provide stable and affordable inputs.  
As elaborated in Table 4 above,  DEFIS+ aligns with the GCF Sector Guide on Agriculture and Food Security 
especially in regard to the investment pathway on Climate Resilien t Agriculture, which focuses on adaptation in 
agriculture and takes into account the mitigation co-benefits of reforestation and renewable energy technologies 
and climate -smart practices used for agriculture and value chains development. It is also aligned with the GCF 
Sector Guide on Ecosystems and Ecosystems services through the pathway on landscape -level protection, and 
restoration to maintain the ability to store and sequester carbon. DEFIS+ aligns with the Sector Guide on Water 
Security through the pathway on strengthening integrated water resources management and water management. 
DEFIS+ targets the most vulnerable areas and livelihoods to the impacts of increasing temperatures and 
increased rainfall variability. The adaptation interventions focus on the production of staple foods, cash crops and 
foods that have high nutritional value,  by making them more resilient to climate change impacts, especially by 
implementing activities with small scale farmers related to improved water management, irrigation and resilient 
agricultural practices and seeds coupled with landscape restoration, and  agro-climatic information systems.

 B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
Government institutions, local communities and agricultural development institutions will be involved in DEFIS+ 
implementation, which will ensure the sustainability of the investments from an institutional standpoint. In addition, 
DEFIS+ will feed into nat ional policy dialogues and strategies, particularly those pertaining to climate change, to 
ensure that the project’s components are integrated into and can inform the country’s legal and policy framework. 
This will further reinforce the synergies between D EFIS+ and the national programs implemented by MINAE and 
MEDD, as well as with the activities led by research organisations, such as FOFIFA.  
DEFIS+ will follow an integrated approach that uses a set of interconnected interventions to generate new 
opportunities for rural communities. This approach will create the enabling conditions for sustainable development 
to ensure that local producers bene fit from increased and long -lasting socio -economic returns as they improve 
their resilience to climate change, reduce vulnerability to climate shocks, food and nutrition insecurity. 
Partnerships between producers and market organisations, as well as the in clusion of smallholder farmers in the 
formal economy will generate social and economic benefits that last well beyond the project’s duration.  
In particular, the  DEFIS+ exit strategy will consider :  
● The establishment of simple and efficient water harvesting systems that require minimal technical skills 
and that can be easily managed and maintained by beneficiaries during and beyond the project’s lifetime. Training 
and awareness -building activities for  the beneficiaries on climate -resilient agricultural practices, including efficient 
water use and management, maintenance of irrigation infrastructure, and value addition to agricultural products. 
The rehabilitation and maintenance of the non -transferable infrastructure are partly financed by users of the hydro -
agricultural network, through their contributions (user fees). In addition, the Regions, annually include expenditure 
projections in their budgets as does the State, through the Fund for the Restoration and Maintenanc e of the 
Irrigations Infrastructure Networks (FRERHA).  
● Proposed DEFIS+ interventions are consistent with the development priorities of the Malagasy 
government. This is important, because it is this consistency that will ensure the institutionalisation of the project 
activities and their sustainability. At the level of the GR 4W development for example, DEFIS+ interventions will 
be linked to the World Bank -funded Madagascar Road Connectivity and the Madagascar Road Sector 
Sustainability projects in Malagasy regions, including Atsimo Atsinanana , which is in the DEFIS+ target  area. 
Thus, these two projects constitute an important baseline investment that DEFIS+ will leverage at various levels, 
including drawing lessons from and building on existing institutional arrangements for road improvements in rural 
Madagascar. DEFIS+ wi ll catalyse but also synergize with these projects in regions where they will overlap for 
enhanced socio -economic and environmental benefits.  
● A maintenance programme for roads, storage and packaging infrastructures, will be established by the 
communities and financed through user contributions/ service payments and Regions budgets. The climate -
resilient nature of the road design and construction will reduce maintenance costs and reinforce the investments’ 
long-term sustainability.  
● By supporting the MFIs, DEFIS+ will develop the partnership of proximity, leveraging the advantages of 
MFIs as local institutions with easier access, simplified procedures and project design and lower interest rates 
compared to other sources of local finan ce such as loan sharks. DEFIS+’s collaboration with MFIs will contribute 
to the development of the MFI sector and reinforce access to finance for agriculture.  
● Women’s access to land will be secured and guaranteed  long after the project ends. This will be done 
based on IFAD past experience and the context, namely depending on the existence or not of land offices and/or 
municipal development plans, the solutions could be, among others: the ex -ante land agreement bet ween the 
WUA  and the Municipality. DEFIS+ will facilitate access to land for women and young people, especially female 
heads of household, in the  new irrigated areas  to be constructed under DEFIS . For existing irrigated areas, 
DEFIS+ will make it a condition of access to funding that women and young people are included among the 
beneficiaries of the land. Awareness -raising and the mobilization of traditional authorities are necessary to 
overcome c ertain traditions that prevent access to land, especially for women heads of household.  
● An Operations and Maintenance Plan will be  included in the Project Implementation Manual (PIM), with 
detailed information on the financial management of key infrastructure, including solar -powered facilities, water -
harvesting systems, and check dams that can guarantee the sustainability of the pro ject. The project will also 
target youth groups and associations to be engaged as entrepreneurs in the supply chain of the different 
technologies including the maintenance of equipment at the local level.   
● One of the critical elements for ensuring sustainability to underpin the exit strategy is capacity 
development. DEFIS+ has been designed with capacity -building interventions  partly for local technicians and 
farmers . In this regard, the project will train 2 000 trainers, actors and technicians  on construction standards that 
enhance road resilience to climate change; 2 500 farmers on the utilisation of new technologies (renewable 
energy and energy -efficient small -scale technologies, water efficient technology); and 45 000 farmers and their


---
*Page 50*
---

B 
 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 46 
 households on nutrition. DEFIS+ will also build capacities of the decentralised government. Developed capacities 
of different stakeholders are part of the exit strategy that will contribute to the project’s sustainability.  
 
Madagascar’s Law 90 -016 places the maintenance, management and control of hydro -agricultural networks in 
the hands of the beneficiaries constituted in WUAs. The Union maintains and protects the primary infrastructure 
common to the WUAs, including  the main canal, the sand trap, and above all the dams. WUAs, maintain the 
secondary infrastructure (secondary, tertiary canals, etc.) specific to one or more WUAs. The "dina" or social 
conventions, which apply to all members of the WUAs, are rules agreed upon  by all members to ensure the  proper 
functioning of the association. For each violation of the “dina”, the damage must be noted, followed by a fine 
which is drawn up and then applied after signature by both parties (the commission and the perpetrator). The exit 
strategy and the respon sibility for the management of DEFIS+ infrastructures are summarized in Table 6.  
Furthermore, in terms of exit strategy, DEFIS+ will support the integration of producers into markets and create 
strong long -lasting relationships of trust between producers and market operators. Two scenarios exist: an 
available product offer or building on an existing market. Depending on whether the products are available or 
build on an existing market, the result will be private actors engaged through aggregation such as cooperatives 
of private producers or the support of Farmer Organisations (FOs) thro ugh local microfinance.

C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)  
C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building 
activities?  Yes ☒    No ☐ 
C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology 
development/transfer?  Yes ☒    No ☐ 
Adapting smallholders and farmers to climate change requires not only building the resilience of production 
structures (irrigated perimeters and adjacent sub -watersheds, rural roads) to climate risks, but also 
strengthening the capacities of farmers and rural development actors to: (i) better analyse production systems, 
constraints and the changes they face; (ii) acquire new knowledg e and learn new farming practices to adapt 
their production systems to climate risks; and (iii) adopt social skills, such as good nutrition and gender 
practices, that can also help them strengthen their resilience to climate change and food and nutrition 
insecurity.  
DEFIS+ capacity building activities are summarised in Table 7a and will focus on: (i) collective management 
of watersheds and irrigated perimeters and their protection against erosion; (ii) efficient water management for 
irrigated perimeters and mechanical  and biological protection measures for watersheds; (iii) introduction of 
agroforestry techniques and climate -resilient species and varieties in dryland upland farming systems through 
the organisation of farmer field schools and exchange visits; (iv) new c onstruction norms linked to climate 
change; (v) training of rural development actors in the use of agrometeorological tools and climate change 
adaptation measures; (vi) household training on nutrition and gender; and (vii) training in DEFIS+ management. 
The cost of investment in capacity building is USD 14,565  million, which is approximately 27% of GCF financing 
(see details in Table 7a below).

For the operation and maintenance of hydro -agricultural infrastructure: at the regional level, a public institution, 
the Regional Hydro -agricultural Network Rehabilitation and Maintenance Fund (RHNRMF), is endowed with 
legal personality and administrative and financial autonomy. The headquarters of the fund is located in the 
capital of the region concerned. The role of each regional RHNRMF is to receive and manage funds intended 
for rehabilitation, technology transfer, maintenance,  and management of all str ategic works in the regional 
territory that are not transferable to other areas. The RHNRMF contributes to the repairs necessary in the event 
of damage caused by floods, cyclones or natural disasters to all the hydro -agricultural networks in each region.
