I. Overall  assessment  of the Secretariat  
1. The funding  proposal  is presented  to the Board  for consideration  with  the following 
remarks:  
 
Strengths  Points  of caution  
This project seeks to alleviate the detrimental 
impacts of climate change by deploying 
sustainable adaptation measures. Leveraging 
valuable insights acquired from pr evious  
projects funded by the International Fund for 
Agricultural Development (IFAD) and the 
ongoing pro -poor agricultural investment 
initiative, Inclusive Agriculture 
Transformation Program me (DEFIS +), 
supported by IFAD, the OPEC Fund for 
International Development, and other 
contributors, the project aims to optimize 
positive experiences to augment its impact 
and ensure investments are resilient to 
climate challenges. The new G CF project , 
known as DEFIS+,  specifically  builds upon the 
existing program.  The project targets 
vulnerable communities in one of regions of 
the world that is most affected by climate  
change.  Throughout the project, it is imperative to 
execute a sustainability strategy. This strategy 
should  encompass a funding blueprint for 
replication, as well as a comprehensive plan to 
incorporate operational and maintenance 
considerations into GCF investments. 
Additionally, it is crucial to investigate 
potential replication opportunities  within 
Africa and Madagascar.  
This project will help to strengthen the 
resilience of the agricultural production system 
and vulnerable marginal communities to the 
impact s of climate change  in drought -affected 
areas by improv ing water management and 
strengthening the resilience of roads by 
applying the concept of “green roads ” for 
water -harvesting  and nature -based solutions .  
IFAD will conduct technical assessments and 
feasibility studies to guide the detailed designs

of climate -resilient infrastructure, such as  
irrigation systems, roads, water management 
schemes, and activities within the agricultural

2. The Board  may  wish  to consider  approving  this funding  proposal  with  the terms  and 
conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVII, titled “List of proposed conditions and 
recommendations”.  
II. Summary  of the Secretariat’s assessment  
2.1 Project background  
3. Madagascar has been ranked the fifth most vulnerable country to climate change in the 
world, and the Notre Dame Global  Adaptation Initiative  index, which ranks the climate 
adaptation performance of 181 countries, has classified Madagascar as one of the countries least 
adapted to cope with climate events (rank ing 172  out of 181 countries1). Madagascar belongs to 
the group of least developed countries, with more than 80  per cent  of the population liv ing 
below the poverty level (USD  2 per day ) and 80  per cent  of the population liv ing in rural zones, 
most of them in very remote areas with limited access to basic infrastructure. The poverty rate 
was 74.3  per cent of the population in 2019, corresponding to an increase of 1.38 million people 
in just one year (2020) , and it is expected  to continue to rise . Madagascar experienced 
unprecedented extreme  and consecutive droughts from 2017 to 2022, affecting crop 
production, food and nutrition security across the country.  The south -west and the extreme 
south are characteri zed by semi -desert environments with erratic and low rainfall of less than 
800 mm per year. A decrease in average annual precipitation of up to 8  per cent  is projected by 
2050. No significant change is expected in terms of average annual rainfall accumulation ; 
however, it is predicted that there will be an increase in the amplitude of inter -annual rainfall 
variability , as well as frequen cy of extre me events. This is particularly true in the project area, 
which is formed of six regions ,  in the south of the country. The great variability of rainfall in the 
project area leads to either droughts or situations where the water balance is very much in 
surplus owing to floods and heavy rains. Temperature s in southern Madagascar are estimated 
to increase by between 1.2 and 2.1  °C by 2050. Under the  Intergovernmental Panel on Climate 
Change representative concentration pathway  (RCP ) 4.5 scenario, the number of days subject to 
extreme heat events is projected to increase from 32.4 to 54.7  by 2050 , posing extreme ly high 
risks to human lives. 
4. The main climate -related changes include (i) a decrease in the amount of annual 
precipitation, with more marked and/or more frequent periods of drought; (ii) a delay in the 
arrival of the rains, which requires adaptation  in terms of the agricultural calendar; (iii) 
increase d intensity of extreme climatic phenomena (cyclones, droughts, floods, storms and 
strong winds); and (iv) a very high temporal and spatial variability in rainfall, which is 
unpredictable. Additionally, smallholder  farm s are frequently exp osed to  outbreaks  of pest s and 
disease which cause significant crop and income losses. Since 2010, the areas most affected by 
drought events are in the project area, particularly the Atsimo -Atsinanana, Vatovavy and 
Fitovinany regions.  
 
1 https://gain.nd.edu/our -work/country -index/rankings/  Accessed on 26 September 2023 .


---
*Page 99*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 99 
 
 
5. The greenhouse gas ( GHG ) emissions in Madagascar come predominantly from the land -
based sectors , namely  agriculture, forestry and land -use change , which together represent 80.5  
per cent  of Madagascar’s national emissions. The emissions are mainly from livestock (enteric 
fermentation and manure) and agriculture (methane from rice cultivation, poor soil 
management, deforestation for agricultural purposes and fertili zer application).  
6. The proposed project, Increase Resilience to Climate Change of  Smallholders Receiving 
the Services of the Inclusive Agricultural Value Chains Programme  (known as DEFIS+) , will help 
to reduc e the negative impact of climate change by applying adaptation measures. It will  build 
on positive lessons learned from previous  International Fund for Agricultural Development  
(IFAD ) projects and from the ongoing pro -poor agricultural investment programme, the 
Inclusive Agriculture Transformation Programme ( DEFIS ), funde d by IFAD and the O PEC  Fund 
for International Development and other donors.  
7. The project will also allow capitalization of the potential of agroforestry, reforestation 
and renewable energy practices  to provide mitigation co -benefits . 
2.2 Component -by-component  analysis  
8. The project will be implemented around the following three complementary outcomes , 
which have  been  strategically conceived to contribute to climate change adaptation , with 
mitigation co -benefits:  
(a) Outcome 1: Strengthened climate resilience of agricultural production systems 
and the enhancement and maintenance of ecosystem services by improving access to 
efficient water management systems for sustainable agriculture, which includes 
enhancing the resilience of 15,000 ha irrigat ion schemes and 8,100 ha subwatersheds  
through mechanical protection and ecosystem -based approaches to restore 5,500 ha 
landscape. In addition, it includes reforestation of degraded and marginal areas and 
sustainable agroforestry  practices; improving access to regular and appropriate agro -
climate data and disaster risk reduction using agro -climatic information systems; 
improving the climate resilience of food production systems for 45,000 small family 
farms through the promotion o f climate -resilient farming technologies and practices and 
integrated pest management techniques; and introducing innovative, low -cost, context -
specific renewable energy and energy -efficient technologies for farming and processing 
(such as solar pumps and dryers) and domestic activities (such as improved cookstoves 
to reduce the use of firewood) , which contribute to reduc ing GHG emissions. The 
economic and financial analysis highlights the climate and environmental benefits 
envisaged by the project ; 
(b) Outcome 2: Increased income generation through improved market accessibility . 
This outcome revolves around promoting “green  roads ” (i.e. climate -resilient rural 
tertiary roads) , supporting the climate resilience of rural infrastructure used for access 
to markets and utilizing  the water -harvesting potential of roads. This includes (i) 
investing in reinforc ement  works , including well -designed cross -drainage systems on 
540 km rural roads to protect the roads from damage due to climate hazards, such as 
floods and heavy rainfall , and to harvest water for drought resilience by efficiently 
managing the water around the roads; (ii) training 2,000 trainers, construction workers 
and professionals on construction and maintenance standards, which will be more 
sustainable than the business -as-usual scenario  in the long  term owing to the reliance 
on local green technolog ies; (iii) i mproving climate resilience by introducing water 
harvesting options for increased water availability for farming, soil moisture


---
*Page 100*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 100 
 
 
improvement and aquifer recharge; and (iv) adopting well -designed climate resilience 
cross -drainage systems to keep the roads functional all year round and not , as at 
present,  being open only during the dry season ( eight to nine  months a year) and closed 
during the rainy season ; and 
(c) Outcome 3: Improved food and nutrition security through managing knowledge o f 
food production systems. Local skills and knowledge will be leveraged , for instance, 
through the development of construction operation manuals , in order to scale up 
innovation. This outcome is inherently linked to outcomes 1 and 2, as it benefits from 
the introduction of climate -smart practices and from increased income generation, both  
of which  result in enhanced food and nutrition security and mitigate the negative impact 
of extreme climate events. Outcome 3 will also promote non -timber forest products and 
neglected and underutili zed climate -tolerant food species as a strategy for adapt ing to 
climate change, reduc ing GHG emissions, reduc ing environmental degradation and 
improv ing agricultural diversification, livelihoods and food security.  
III. Assessment  of performance  against  investment  criteria  
3.1 Impact  potential                                                            Scale:  Medium  to high  
9. DEFIS+ proposes a comprehensive approach to sustainably improving resilience to 
climate shocks and enhancing nutrition and food security for vulnerable households in the 
target areas. DEFIS+ will enhance the capacity of agricultural systems and vulnerable rural 
communities to adapt to the adverse impacts of climate change so that farmers are better able 
to confront the increasing frequency and intensity of drought s and changes in rainfall patterns 
that often result in floods. This requires bringing  up to da te current agricultural production 
systems that are dependent on rainfall and whose practices and infrastructures are no longer 
appropriate in the context of climate change. DEFIS+ proposes to reduce smallholder farmers’ 
exposure to climate and economic shoc ks by “climate  proofing ” irrigation infrastructure and 
rural roads to increase agricultural water availability, reduce the risk of market access 
disruptions caused by extreme climate events and promote and disseminate climate -smart  
agricultural practices . DEFIS+ will benefit  447,20 1 people , 50 per cent of wh om are women.  
10. At the field level, introducing  water -saving technolog ies such as the implementation of 
drip -irrigation systems and the system of rice intensification , will improve the availability of 
water, reduce vulnerability to water shortages and increase productivity per hectare. This will 
increase the  area of  recoverable crops with a view to increasing  levels of production. The 
support provided by DEFIS+ , through the dissemination of drought - and stress -tolerant crop 
varieties and the adoption of integrated pest management methods and other improved 
agronomic practices , will contribute to further reducing production risk and sustainably 
increasing productivity. Th e resilience of pr oduction systems will be strengthened by investing 
in ecosystem restoration , including the promotion of an integrated watershed and catchment 
management approach. This reduces the risk of erosion and will improve the sustained 
productivity of farms through the retention of fertile topsoils.  DEFIS+ will also promote low -cost 
on- and off -farm renewable energy and energy -efficient technologies, such as improved energy -
efficient cookstoves and handmade briquettes , which will contribute towards  reducing forest 
degradation . Other technologies to be promoted include solar pumps for irrigation, biogas 
production along with livestock systems,  and solar milling and dryers for product processing. 
These technologies will contribute to improv ing efficiency and production  and lowering GHG 
emissions . They will also contribut e to reducing women’s workload  and time burden (i.e. the


---
*Page 101*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 101 
 
 
amount of time spent on unpaid domestic work such as fetching water) , with  a positive  impact  
on the health, education  and nutrition status of women and, indirectly , their young children.   
11. Through these interventions, it is estimated that the project  will directly improve the 
climate resilience of 447,20 1 individual beneficiaries and indirectly impact the lives of 
3,543,719  beneficiaries in the six regions targeted by DEFIS+ , improving their ability to 
sustainably combat the negative effects of climate change while also helping them to produce 
greater yields and gain access to markets. This results in greater income generation for rural 
communities and reduced dependence on the exploitation o f natural resources in lean p eriods. 
With the associated ecosystem regeneration activities implemented under the project , this will 
establish  a positive feedback loop that will result in a greater impact on ecosystem restoration , 
while additional increases  in biomass  will have an associated reduction in GHG emissions and 
an increase in the carbon storage capacity of the targeted ecosystems.   
3.2 Paradigm  shift  potential                                                                     Scale:  High 
12. The project constitutes an important step in a longer -term process of transforming 
agriculture in Madagascar. The required behavioural changes among a large and poor 
population highly dependent on agricultural output constitute an enormous task that the 
Government cannot achieve on its own. Several  existing projects in the south of Madagascar will 
benefit from the DEFIS+ project through its contribut ion to transforming the climate -vulnerable 
and unsustainable agricultur e sector into a green and sustainable climate -resilient, market -
oriented  and profitable economic sector  with a modern infrastructure , which also significantly 
contributes to mitigating climate shocks and improving food and nutrition, security, gender 
equality and empowerment. The structural and capacity transformation will be achieved by 
addressing vulnerabilities to climate change and  supporting all stakeholders in improv ing their 
resilience, efficiency and links to markets. The paradigm shift of DEFIS+  includes  the following : 
(a) Climate proofing the production - and market -related infrastructure to improve its 
resilience. The integrated watershed and catchment management approach will scale up 
national experience s in bottom -up participatory watershed and catchment management 
with the dual aims of protecting natural resources and increasing income. Community -
driven sustainable land and water management plans and activities will be supported. 
The planning process, which includes social engineering for behavioural change and 
implementa tion of the plans , will also facilitate knowledge -sharing and joint learning 
among peers in the water users association s and the farmer field schools ; 
(b) The partnerships mobili zed for DEFIS+, the scale of productive investments planned for 
sustainable agricultural development and the focus on building local capacities , together 
constitute a major paradigm shift in regions of southern Madagascar  where  
humanitarian and emergency assistance  is needed . The focus on scaling up well -tested 
approaches and technologies , building on innovative approaches and lessons learned 
from previous IFAD investments (including DEFIS and other IFAD projects in the 
country) , is an important feature of the proposed interventions ; 
(c) Access to stress -tolerant ( regarding drought, heat, flooding, salinity, etc.) and 
nutritionally enhanced varieties and planting materials developed by research centres. 
The production, multiplication and dissemination at the community level of these 
climate -resilient technologies will be promoted , as will the  adoption of integrated pest 
management and other agroecological approaches. Supporting national research centres 
and the  farmer field schools  will create an  enabling environment for improved seeds 
prom otion and adoption by the smallholders ;


---
*Page 102*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 102 
 
 
(d) Greening the road infrastructure will have an overall positive impact on agriculture, 
water availability and soil erosion associated with running surface water , while  having a 
minimal environmental impact and delivering large benefits for civil society ; and 
(e) The project will use a decision support system  to understand risk ( e.g. using hazard 
exposure and vulnerability  to assess the dynamic nature of impact scenarios for crops, 
land use and agri -infrastructure ), including gender -related considerations and 
applications such as Météo Madagascar2and others provided by the Regional 
Meteorological Directorate.  
13. The project will shift agriculture in the country’s target areas from its current situation 
of high vulnerability toward s an alternative paradigm where by better information and climate -
smart farming practices will significantly increase resilience to climate change. The use of state -
of-the-art geographic  information system tools will support strategic planning through 
systematic data -collection and management , which will act as a springboard for science -based 
decision -making processes.  
14. The approach and the tools to be utili zed constitute a package that could be easily 
replicated in other systems and geographic areas in Madagascar or o ther countr ies. The 
successful implementation of this project will result in a new, replicable, long -term and 
sustainable funding model that could enhance climate resilience,  improve conservation, reduce 
land degradation and boost economic development. Outside Madagascar, the proposed model of 
a land -use transition investment fund is suit able  for the most vulnerable African countries, 
where unsustainable agriculture takes up an increasing share of available land and result s in 
deforestation, land degradation and an agric ultur e sector that is not resilient to climate change. 
Thus, this model could be replicated in (but not limited to ) other least developed countries in 
Africa that need large -scale climate investments in rural areas in order to finance the activities 
necessary to achieve the objectives set in their nationally deter mined contributions  and in their 
national adaptation program mes. 
3.3 Sustainable  development  potential                                             Scale:  High  
15. The project  will be implemented by the Government of Madagascar, particularly the 
Ministry of Agriculture  and Livestock (MINAE)  and the Ministry of Economy and Finan ce (MEF ) 
in close synergy with other rural and agricultural development investment  project s operating in 
the southern regions of Madagascar. Similar to other IFAD -funded projects, the interventions 
will rest on using national systems at both the central and the decentrali zed level, and on 
strengthening the capacity of targeted poor smallholders and their l ocal institutions , such as 
farmers’ organi zations, in order to better empower the rural poor, with a particular focus on 
women and youth. When looking at ensuring sustainable development, climate information 
represents an essential element for increasing the capacity of vulnerable households to ada pt to 
climate variability.  
16. The sustainable development benefits include the following:  
(a) Environmental co -benefits.  DEFIS+ will promote sustainable and climate -resilient 
agricultural practices, technologies and infrastructures that contribute to improving the 
efficient management and use of water resources in agriculture, enhancing soil quality 
and restoring degraded l ands. Its integrated watershed management approach will help

Page 103 
 
 
to increase moisture content in the soil, optimize the use of water and expand the 
vegetative cover of degraded lands, while reducing their wind - and rainfall -induced 
erosion ; 
(b) Social co -benefits , including health impacts.  Improved food security and nutrition 
will be among the key social co -benefits that will arise. The proportion of targeted 
households with sufficient food intake is expected to increase from 50 to 90  per cent  by 
the end of the project . In addition, 170,000 households will benefit from nutritional 
education, which will improve the health of women and infants. Better access to water, 
more effective watershed management and the use of off -farm technologies will redu ce 
women’s workload  and time burden . Employment and employability of women and 
youth will increase, and women’s participation in the rural economy will be formali zed 
and recognized ; 
(c) Economic co -benefits.  DEFIS+ will contribute to reduc ing poverty by supporting the 
diversification of agricultural production, building the climate resilience of production 
systems and infrastructures and creating market linkages, which will reduce transaction 
costs and boost the local economy. The sustainable management of agricultural water 
within an integrated watershed approach, coupled with more climate - and stress -
resilient seeds, will sustainably increase agricultural productivity ; 
(d) Gender -sensitive development impact.  DEFIS+ will implement a gender strategy to 
ensure that women’s specific needs are taken into account at all stages and will provide 
opportunities to improve their quality of life, reduce their vulnerability to climate 
change and increase their incomes. The activities proposed for women and youth will 
include t raining farming households on gender issues, and measures to increase their 
access to productive capital and income -generating activities (primarily access to land ), 
with a view to positioning women as rural entrepreneurs and service providers in 
development hubs and facilitat ing their access to capacity -building and financial 
services. The number of green jobs created through this project will have a target 
women headed households.  The project will also involve minorities, vulnerable people 
and people of lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender or other identi ties; and  
(e) Social equity.  The overall strategy of DEFIS+ is to ensure social equity and cohesion 
among all members of civil society. Thus, this project follows an inclusive targeting 
strategy to reach vulnerable groups and poor households headed by women and youth, 
so that these t argeted rural populations, characteri zed by their vulnerability to the 
effects of climate change, will have equal opportunities to access factors of production 
(land, water, inputs, financing, etc.) in order to ensure fair and equitable resul ts and 
enable sustainable impacts on living conditions.  
3.4 Needs  of the recipient                                                                Scale:  High  
17. Madagascar is among the poorest  countries  in the world . Poverty in Madagascar  has 
increased significantly  over the past decade . The poverty rate was estimated to rise to 77.4  per 
cent  of the population in 2020, up from 74.3  per cent in 2019, corresponding to an increase  in 
the population  of 1.38 million in one year. . Poverty is clearly more severe in rural areas, where 
it affects 87.6  per cent of the population, in comparison with 55.1  per cent  in urban areas. 
Extreme poverty is more pronounced in the south. The activities of DEFIS+ aim to restor e 
and/or rehabilitate the soil and improve land use through a participatory approach with the 
local community. This approach will ensure that any potential land conflict is identified and 
resolved before any activities commence. For the rehabilitation of r oads, in the exceptional


---
*Page 104*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 104 
 
 
circumstances of beneficiaries who may be voluntarily displaced , the project will have a 
resettlement policy to be implemented before the works are initiated. Adherence to the IFAD 
Social , Environment al and Climate Assessment Procedures follows the principles of free, prior 
and informed consent and ensures that existing inequalities are not exacerbated . A grievance 
redress mechanism is also articulated for the project to manage any potential conflicts. In 
addition, DEFIS+ fully takes into account the needs of the recipient, which are in line with the 
national priorities.  
3.5 Country  ownership                                                        Scale:  Medium to high 
18. This project is set to provide substantial support to the national policies concerning 
climate change, environmental conservation and sustainable development, along with aligning 
with national strategies (e.g.  the Sectoral Program for Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries ) in a 
direct manner, and to reinforce the National Policy for Action on Climate Change by enhancing 
climate change adaptation efforts, while taking the country ’s unique requirements into 
consideration. Additionally, it will actively facilitate the integration of climate change 
considerations into all tiers of policymaking, as well as within research, development and 
technology transfer in this particular domain.  
19. Madagascar is in the early stages of conceptualizing a GCF country program me using a 
GCF/U nited Nations Environment Programme  readiness grant. Notably, DEFIS+ investments 
harmonize completely with the critical priority sectors outlined in Madagascar ’s national 
adaptation programme of action , namely infrastructure, water resource management, 
agriculture and livestock. These investments will play a direct role in advancing Madagascar ’s 
nationally determined contribution, which primarily seek s to bolster the c ountry ’s resilience 
against cyclones, floods and droughts. The project places significant emphasis on adaptation 
interventions by 2030, encompassing initiatives such as  the widespread adoption of robust 
agricultural models and sustainable and integrated water management (including the utilization 
of surface water resources in semi -arid zones) and the enhancement of the irrigation 
infrastructure to cope with diminished run -off during dry periods.  
20. Furthermore, the project aims to decrease GHG  emissions and enhance carbon 
sequestration, while also focusing on enhancing rice farming systems (specifically “system of 
rice intensification ” (SRI) techniques) and executing ecosystem -based practices for adaptation . 
These solutions encompass , among other things,  alternatives to traditional charcoal use, fossil 
fuels and water abstraction systems.  
21. The national designated authority  of Madagascar , the Ministry of Environment, Ecology  
and Forests , also fully support s the project and has already issued a no-objection letter for this 
project.   
3.6 Efficiency  and effectiveness                                     Scale:  Medium  to high  
22. The overall approach adopted by DEFIS+ is designed to increase efficiency and 
effectiveness by focusing on the set of structural investments associated with the introduction 
of improved technologies and climate -resilient  practices as well as capacity -building of local 
institutions that will leverage growth in targeted agricultural areas. Th is approach , where  
science and engineering are integrated  into in-depth modelling and understanding of 
agricultural risk , will inform the structural investment s through the studies that will be 
undertaken. Furthermore, in order to move from low -yield subsistence farming to market -


---
*Page 105*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 105 
 
 
oriented agriculture, the project ’s theory of change is based on improving the productivity and 
adaptive capacity of production systems to climate change and beneficiaries’ access to markets 
under favourable conditions. In this regard , the theory of change requires significant 
investments in basic infrastructure, so priority will be given to the developm ent of irrigation 
infrastructure and rehabilitation of roads to reduce the risks and costs of accessing the nearest 
markets.  
23. The main benefits  expected  for people in the DEFIS+ project area are (i) creat ion of 
climate -resilient rural communities; (ii) diversification of income sources for the target 
populations, especially young people and women, and creation of green jobs in the project area; 
(iii) reduced transportation costs through rural road rehabilitation, water storage infrastructure 
and market access; (iv) increased revenues for poor people; (v) improved food security, diet 
quality and diversification and nutrition; and, above all (vi) greater awareness of and protection 
from the climate change impacts negatively affecting the livelihoods of the rural population.  
24. IFAD conducted an  economic and financial analysis, with ten proposed interventions 
being modelled and appraised.  
25. The analysis found that aggregate incremental results of the ten interventions combined 
is a financial net present value (NPV) of USD 31.07 million (over 20 years at 8.5  per cent  
financial discount rate) whereas the financial internal rate of return (IRR) and the modified IRR 
are 19 and 9  per cent , respectively. The se incremental aggregate results point towards the 
financial viability of the proposed interventions because the incremental financial NPV is 
positive and the incremental financial IRR remains above the chosen financial discount rate of 
8.5 per  cent. ,  
26. The aggregate economic incremental results are also promising , with the aggregate 
incremental economic NPV estimated at USD 255.57 (over 20 years at 6  per cent discount rate) 
and the economic IRR and modified IRR at 24 and 11  per cent, respectively. Further detailed 
estimates on a per -model basis are presented in the t able below.  
27. The sensitivity analysis results suggest that the obtained economic NPV  and economic 
IRR remain robust and relatively insensitive to cost increase and benefit decrease. 
Consequently, it is expected that the proposed interventions will provide measurable benefits to 
the entire economy of Madagascar, improving the well -being of the environment and intended 
beneficiaries.

IV. Assessment  of consistency  with  GCF  safeguards  and  policies


---
*Page 106*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 106 
 
 
4.1 Environmental  and social  safeguards  
28. Project background . The project aims to strengthen the resilience of agricultural 
production systems and vulnerable communities to the impacts of climate change while 
contributing to emission reduction in six regions in Madagascar through  the dissemination of 
climate -friendly agricultural and livestock management practices , the rehabilitation of road and 
irrigation infrastructure and watersheds , and the introduction of energy -efficient technologies 
while strengthening climate information systems and capacities of institutions through the 
adoption of best practices and knowledge on climate change adaptation . The environmental and 
social co -benefits include the improvement of agricultural water resources , enhanced soil 
quality and restored degraded lands. The project is also expected to increase soil moisture 
content and expand vegetative cover , reducing soil erosion and siltation of water bodies such as 
hydro -agricultural and irr igation canal infrastructure. It is envisaged that the project will 
increase biodiversity in the target areas and surrounding landscapes and maintain the 
important ecological services that it will provide. The social co -benefits include improved health 
and nutrition as a result of better access to water and increased and diversified income sources.  
29. Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments . The accredited 
entity (AE) , IFAD , has assigned a category B environmental and social risk rating to the project , 
given that it will have limited adverse environmental and social risks and/or impacts that are 
site-specific and can be readily mitigated. The Secretariat agrees with the risk categorization 
and it is within the level for which the AE is accredited. The AE has prepared an environmental 
and social management framework (ESMF) for the project that describe s the potential 
environmental and/or social risks/impacts of the project and the measures to address them. 
The ESMF also outline s how different subproject activities would be screened for environmental 
and social risk s/impacts during project implementation , which mitigation measures would be 
applied based on the risk level , and whether site -specific assessments and management plans 
would be required for these subactivities.  
30. Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards . The 
environmental and social risks and/or impacts of the project and the proposed mitigation 
measures  are described below . 
31. ESS 1 : Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and 
impacts . The ESMF describe s the baseline conditions for the targeted areas where project 
activities will take place to meet the project objective of increasing the resilience of rural 
communities to the impacts of climate change. The ESMF describe s all subproject activities in 
detail and list s the potential direct and indirect impacts of each of the activities. It also propose s 
generic , standardized mitigation measures to avoid , minimize  and mitigate such impacts. Most 
of the risks and impacts will be moderate  in scale and can be readily managed and mitigated 
through the appropriate procedures and plans described in the ESMF. Site -specific , tailored and 
more detailed mitigation measures will be developed for subproject activities with a moderate 
risk , which will require site -specific assessment and plans for these activities to be prepared 
during implementation. The ESMF also include s a monitoring protocol and a budget for its 
implementati on. 
32. ESS 2 : Labour and working conditions . The ESMF cover s the impacts on labour and 
working conditions. It include s discussions on the typology of the workers who will be hired 
under the project and the key labour risks and impacts for these workers , including working 
conditions and terms of employment. Occupational health and safety concerns will be managed 
by equipping workers with personal protective equipment adapted to the types of work in 
which they are engaged. Staff will also be trained and educated on the risks and dangers o f 
construction sites. Given th at the project will use solar photovoltaic systems  in its activities , the


---
*Page 107*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 107 
 
 
AE will ensure that there will be no use of forced labour/child labour in the primary supply 
chain of such systems . Labour and working conditions will be aligned to  the AE’s Social , 
Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures , which set out labour management 
principles and the grievance mechanism. Contract documents will also include clauses on labour 
conditions , including zero tolerance of sexual exploitation , abuse and harassment ( SEAH ), and 
the bidding documents for the solar photovoltaic component will  include clauses that ensure  
that  goods are procured from ethical sources.  
33. ESS 3 : Resource efficiency and pollution prevention . The ESMF identifie s the 
activities that generate emissions to air and discharges to water , including possible 
contamination of land and generation of associated wastes , hazardous materials and pesticides , 
as well as activities expected to utilize natural resources , including water and energy. 
Atmospheric pollution , dust emissions and noise pollution from traffic (e.g. construction 
machinery and vehicles ) are expected. Soil compaction and increased risk of erosion due to 
clearing of vegetation for the construction facility , as well as soil pollution by infiltration or 
leaching of pollutants (e.g. fuel storage) , may occur. Alteration of the quality of water resources 
through wastewater discharges from the construction site , sedimentation in irrigation canals 
and pollution of water bodies through  the use of agricultural inputs may occur. To mitigate 
these , several measures are planned, such as effluent treatment prior to discharge , proper 
storage of fuels and carrying out of proper maintenance of equipment and machiner y. 
Implement ation of a site waste management plan is also envisaged. Screening on resource 
efficiency and pollution prevention to assess these risks and impacts in a site -specific manner 
will be conducted  during implementation  and site -specific management plans will be prepared 
that are tailored for subproject activities.  
34. ESS 4 : Community health , safety and security . The ESMF describe s the activities that 
will generate risks and impacts to the health and safety of communities. These are mainly 
associated with construction activities , which include risk of accidents to local populations 
associated with the use of vehicles and construction machiner y, disturbance due to dust 
emissions , and nuisance noise and disruption of rural activities. The measures contemplated 
include carrying out regular maintenance of equipment , vehicles and machiner y, ensuring that 
base camps and project activities are situated far from the villages and work is done during the 
daytime only , and conduct ing appropriate information and awareness campaigns for local 
populations on the duration of the activities to be carried out in the area. Existing social conflicts 
regard ing use of agricultural land , water use and infrastructure maintenance activities between 
water user associations are expected to be reduced by implementing the activities of the project 
(e.g. outcome  1), which encompass improvements to agricultural lan d and food production and 
water resource management systems through participatory consultation , better procedures and 
regulations , an established maintenance plan and enhanced institutional capacities (such as 
adoption of a conflict prevention and resolution mechanism).  
35. ESS 5 : Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement . The project will avoid or 
minimize any resettlement or economic displacement that could have a negative impact on the 
populations concerned. The AE has also assessed the project as having  a low probability of 
resettlement and economic displacement. However , should people lose their land and means of 
livelihood , compensation will be provided. Eligibility criteria , monetary , crop , land replacement 
and compensation scales , and an institutional arrangement for implementation of an action plan 
will be drawn up. There  will also be a mechanism for consultation with and participation of 
beneficiaries in the planning , information dissemination , execution and monitoring , follow -up 
and implementat ion of the compensation and mitigation plan (i.e. abbreviated resettlement 
action plan) , which could be prepared and implemented in accordance with the provisions of 
the AE’s Social , Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures .


---
*Page 108*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 108 
 
 
36. ESS 6 : Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resources.  The project’s construction and rehabilitation activities could result in loss of 
vegetation and disturbance of wildlife through  the destruction of natural habitats , and pose a 
risk of possible encroachment on sensitive areas. The large -scale distribution of certain 
varieties of seeds can also lead to a reduction in floristic biodiversity. Further more , there is a 
risk of proliferation of invasive plants in the watershed protection -related activities  of the 
project . To mitigate these , the project intends to prioritize the use of indigenous species , comply 
with forestry legislation and limit disturbance to the areas that are required for the 
implementation of the subprojects  (such as avoiding infringing on protected natural sites and 
critical habitats or areas with significant biodiversity). The project activities will also involve 
conduct ing preliminary studies to analyse each variety to be introduced to anticipate their 
responses in the field based on the type of environmental/pedoclimatic condition , and 
conduct ing quarantine checks and follow national guidelines in the introduction of new 
germplasm. Reforestation of different species to promote biodiversity will also be imp lemented. 
The project will not support activities  that are  prohibited by host  country legislation or 
international conventions relating to the protection of biodiversity resources , cultural heritage 
or other legally protected areas.  
37. Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples . The AE confirms that 
there are no Indigenous Peoples in the areas targeted by the project ; however , it notes that good 
practices , including recognizing cultural heritage and identi ty as assets , obtaining free , prior  and 
informed consent , strengthening community -driven development , promoting equitable access 
to lands , territories and resources and valuing Indigenous knowledge , will be developed 
through the project. Stakeholder consultations have also echoed the need for valori zing 
traditional knowledge , a recommendation in line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and on 
which the AE can consult with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group , as part of its role 
and function.  
38. ESS 8 : Cultural heritage . Although adverse impacts are not expected on physical 
cultural heritage , given that the project will only renovate existing roads and irrigation 
structures and work with farmers/local governments to support climate -resilient agricultural 
practices , the construction works on such infrastructure may potentially affect culturally 
relevant sites through accidental encounters. In such cases , the project will implement  the 
“chance find ” procedures contained in the ESMF and comply with th e national ordinance on 
protection and conservation of national heritage.  
39. Implementation arrangements.  The ESMF outline s the basic responsibilities for 
implementing arrangements and divide s responsibility between the project management unit at 
MINAE , the ESS team in the project management unit , and that of the contractors /implementing 
parties . It specifie s the implementation and monitoring of mitigation measures for the potential 
impacts of the project , including the frequency of monitoring of performance indicators. The ESS 
team will be responsible for monitoring the environment al and social aspects of the project 
during its implementation. Staff training and capacity -building of its project partners on 
environmental and social issues , as well as the verification of compliance of the project with 
environmental and social performance indicators , will be implemented and monitored.  
40. Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism . Public consultations 
have been held with stakeholders in the target regions , including administrative authorities , 
various local partners and representatives of the beneficiaries and local communities to be 
affected by the project. The stakeholder engagement plan provided a description of how 
stakeholder engagement will take place during the subproject planning , implementation 
monitoring and evaluation phases. The stakeholder engagement plan  also describe s how project 
information will be disclosed by the AE and  the Government of Madagascar through  MEF and


---
*Page 109*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 109 
 
 
MINAE, the project’s executing entity  (EE), during the preparation and implementation phases , 
using differentiated mechanisms to reach different stakeholders.  
41. The project proposal also describe s two grievance redress mechanisms : the institutional 
grievance  redress  mechanism of the AE  and the project -level grievance  redress  mechanism. 
Both have various channels  through which  grievances may be submitted and general processes 
for managing grievances. At the AE level , grievances on non -compliance with environmental and 
social policies can be submitted by email or post and will be managed by the IFAD  Regional 
Division. Complainants not satisfied with the response can appeal to the Vice -President  of IFAD . 
At the project  level , grievances may be submitted by email or in person and will be handled by 
the project management unit.  In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy , the GCF 
Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage , including before a 
claim has been made.  
42. SEAH . The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM -
2021/18 requires safeguarding from SEAH in GCF -financed activities. The ESMF of the DEFIS+ 
project outlines SEAH risks areas and proposes mitigation measures , linking the address ing of 
SEAH with the gender -based violence ( GBV ) risks identified in the gender assessment and its 
gender activity  plan . In order to address SEAH , the project will , among other things , strengthen 
awareness of GBV and SEAH issues among staff and project be neficiaries; develop an 
information/awareness protocol on SEAH; strengthen GBV/SEAH operational procedures for 
projects , including a code of conduct for public works contractors; conduct various information 
and awareness -raising activities for direct and indirect project beneficiaries; and ensure that 
contractor contracts and collaboration agreements have relevant SEAH clauses. The AE has also 
proposed changes to the SEAH grievance redress mechanism in alignment with the GBV code of 
conduct , and various rep orting platforms , namely  online or by telephone or mail , and in -person 
reporting. The Secretariat  recommend s that the AE ensure that in the event that SEAH occurs , 
the reporting and SEAH complaints handling is survivor centred and as gender responsive as 
possible. Overall , the SEAH safeguarding measures presented by the AE are in alignment with 
GCF SEAH provisions .  
4.2 Gender  policy  
43. The AE has provided  a gender  assessment  and gender  action  plan , there by compl ying  
with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.  
44. The gender assessment describes the existing enabling environment that provides the 
space to address gender inequality and work towards the increased empowerment of women. 
The constitution of Madagascar enshrines equal rights between women and men; subsequent 
amendments to legal and regulatory systems were made to ensure that equality takes root in 
society. Further more , Madagascar has signed and ratified the Convention on the Eliminat ion of 
All Forms of  Discrimination Against Women and other international t reati es and many of its 
national laws and existing national polic ies and legislation support the equality of women. 
Madagascar has developed a National Policy for the Promotion of Women , which  aims at 
addressing gender inequality in incomes and economy , education and training gaps , rights to 
health , reproductive rights and decision -making powers of women while also improving 
institutional mechanisms. Despite the intent and span of effort , in practice ensuring equality has 
been and remains  a challenge. Land -related issues , particularly ownership and inheritance , pose 
a challenge for women , while access to productive resources and assets is equally challenging 
for women because of inherent restrictive norms and practices.


---
*Page 110*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 110 
 
 
45. The challenge to translate policies and legal intent into practice has resulted in women 
in Madagascar facing an added burden with respect to the  impacts of climate change. The 
gender assessment , which was undertaken with stakeholder consultations and a desk review , 
describes the inequality felt by women. Regarding land and factors of production , women do not 
have th e right to own and make decision s on land ; neither are they allowed in most cases to 
inherit it. If they need to have access to land , it is only available  in the form of renting or if they 
are part of a women’s group. Access to land in such a manner does not give women the right to 
decide on long -term investments , even to plan t trees. Female -headed households might own 
land but is generally marginal , , less productive  land. Limited  access to inputs such as seeds , 
finance , fertilizers and irrigation infrastructure and low levels of skills restrict s women’s ability 
to meet their daily needs  or mitigate against these  limitations  as well as the compounded effects 
of climate change induced realities. These  challenges affect women’s ability to generate 
lucrative activities and revenue/incomes. This does not , however , mean that women do not 
contribute to the agricultur al economy; they do , but do so within the confines of the realities 
expressed above. The situation of women is similar regarding the use of forest and other  natural 
resources , upon which they depend a great deal for making traditional medicine and fulfilling 
other needs.  Men use forest  resources  in the same way , including for cooking and timber for 
construction. The impacts of overuse and misuse affect  all. Decision s on the use of these 
resources are made  by men , with  a limited role for women in the decision -making structures. 
The burden on women is exacerbated by climate change in that it contributes to an increased 
workload , food insecurity , longer  fuelwood and water collection times , with more  time needed 
by women to fulfil their roles. In addition,  the assessment indicates that violence against women 
is a challenge in the project area , and notes  the importan ce of keep ing this in mind during the 
course of project implementation to ensur e that project interventions do not allow  the 
perpetuation of GBV within the programming area and possibly  beyond . 
46. The AE has provided a gender action plan that is targeted at addressing the gender 
issues identified in the gender assessment. The gender action plan contains a set of activities 
with corresponding targets and indicators. The AE has also provided a budget to ensure the 
effective implementation of the actions identified and a timeline within which each of the 
activities will be undertaken.  
47. With a view to building on existing capabilities of women and providing support to 
address identified gaps the project will target the following four areas of vulnerability for 
women (including female -headed household and youth ): (i) limited inclusion and participation 
of women; (ii) limited empowerment of women and youth in the agricultural value chain; (iii) 
limited opportunities to improve their conditions within the community; and (iv) limited 
capacity -building and development opportunities rendering women less resilient and affecting 
their role and decision -making power. The gender issues identified will be addressed through 
various project components , such as in the reconstruction of irrigation schemes  which will have 
benefits t o women in terms of food diversification , bringing sufficient quantities of good -quality 
water closer to their homes (after consultative processes); engagement in water user 
associations; and addressing unde rlying social norms using community -led discussions to equal 
access and opportunities for women and girls. Community -led discussion s, besides being 
expected to improve gender relations , are expected to be a platform that will encourage 
awareness -raising and adoption of good climate cha nge practices. The project will also provide 
opportunities for women to develop leadership skill s to ensure that they are able to engage and 
have a say in decisions that affect their life and livelihoods , such as in water user associations. 
To ensure women’s participation and inclusion in the various activities , opportunities will be 
taken to organize women -only events , to institute quota systems  and to provide targeted 
training for women in , for example , functional literacy and leadership skills. Furthermore , there 
will be ample opportunit ies for women to engage in and benefit from the activities of t he project 
which focus on sustainable watershed management and restoration of the landscape , including


---
*Page 111*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 111 
 
 
in increasing their representation at the leadership and decision -making level. The project will 
work to ensure that women and youth get access to and benefit from tailored knowledge (based 
on information on needs and preference in accessing) , productive capital , climate insurance , 
green jobs (in the reconstruction and maintenance works through mandatory recruitment 
procedures with equal pay for equal work , while also encouraging women -owned construction 
enterprises  coming into the workstream ) and engagemen t in income -generating activities. 
Access to land , which is a major barrier to women , will be addressed through allocation and 
quota systems for women and youth. This will be one of the lessons to be learned and one of the 
main tasks of DEFIS+ , which  will facilitate access to land for women and youth through the 
development of new irrigated areas ; while for existing areas , the project will set out the 
inclusion of women and youth as a condition for access to funding. At the same time , the project 
will invest in awareness -raising and mobilization of traditional authorities , to address and 
overcome certain practices that prevent access to land , especially for women heads of 
household.  
48. The project’s national steering committee  (NSC)  will include the Ministry  of Social 
Protection and Gender and will have a gender expert to monitor the day -to-day implementation , 
monitoring and evaluation of the project , while the project management unit  and the project 
monitoring and evaluation  systems will be charged with ensuring the monitoring of the 
implementation of the gender action plan.  
4.3 Risks  
4.3.1.  Overall  project  assessment  (medium  risk)  
49. This funding proposal is a  100  per cent adaptation project to strengthen  the climate 
resilience of the agricultural production system and vulnerable communities in Madagascar. 
The project will implement land -based activities related to agroforestry , reforestation and 
renewable energy practices , which will be measured as co -benefits . 
50. GCF is considering a loan of USD 12.8 million and grant of USD 41.0 million together 
with co -financing of USD 127.5  million in loan and grant from IFAD. The c o-financing ratio is 
1:2.4 and accounts for 70 per cent of total financin g. 
4.3.2.  Accredited  entity/executing  entity  capability  to execute  the current  project  
(medium risk)  
51. IFAD is an international AE with over 40 years of development work in global 
agriculture and food security. To date , IFAD has provided over USD 23 billion in grants and low  
interest rate loan s. The Government of Madagascar , through MEF and MINAE , is the project EE 
and the funding agreement will be implemented through both  MEF and MINAE. MEF will 
manage , control and evaluate external funds while MINAE will enter into contracts for  the 
procurement of services for project activities. IFAD has an extensive hist ory of working with 
MINAE in implementing projects and program mes. 
4.3.3.  Project -specific  execution  risks  (medium  risk)  
52. The funding proposal will expose GCF to several country -related risk issues in 
Madagascar and its low sovereign rating of B – (Standard & Poor’s ). Madagascar ’s rating is due in


---
*Page 112*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 112 
 
 
part to the government's large budget deficit , low-income  level s and the low level of economic 
development. The country ha d a reported gross domestic product per capita of USD 453 in 
2022. Madagascar also reported a sharp economic contraction following the onset of the 
coronavirus disease pandemic in 2020. Flooding , drought and other climate change induced 
natural disaster s have also negatively affected the country's economy. However , Madagascar 
enjoys strong relations with multilateral institutions that provide concessional and grant 
funding support , includ ing support for infra structure projects to address energy and transport 
development and donations for food insecurity. In addition , an International Monetary Fund  
extended credit facility should continue to help to boost economic growth by increasing 
investment and social spending as well as provide policy support to strengthen governance and 
anti -corruption efforts.  
4.3.4.  Project viability and concessionality  
53. The proposed project will build on the IFAD existing agricultural investment 
programme , DEFIS , which was approved in 2017 and funded by IFAD , the OPEC Fund for 
International Development and other donors. To strengthen the climate resilience of small 
farmers , the project will , among other activities , strengthen irrigation infrastructure to increase 
water availability , build the resilience of tertiary rural roads to reduce market access disruption , 
and allow access to basic services during periods of excessiv e rainfall . The requested 
participation from GCF will represent approximately 30 per cent of total programme size in a 
partly loan - and grant -funded project. The level of concessionality is reasonable given the co-
financing of 70 per cent  from IFAD. It is also noted that the larger proportion of grant financing 
compared with loans in GCF funding is justified in view of  Madagascar ’s weak sovereign rating. 
The country has been heavily reliant on support from multilateral institutions , and it will be 
appropriate for GCF  to align its participation to the borrower ’s credit condition.  
4.3.5.  GCF  portfolio  concentration  risk  (low  risk)  
54. In the case  of approval , the impact  of this proposal  on the GCF  portfolio  concentration  in 
terms of result s area and single proposal is not material.  
4.3.6.  Compliance risk ( medium  risk ) 
55. This program me will mainly address the agricultur e sector.  In doing so , there is likely to 
be significant expenditure at the subnational or local level and among a wide variety of 
beneficiaries. While the expenditure that wil l benefit individuals in terms of technical and other 
assistance do es not appear to be unusually large , there will still need to be monitoring and 
assurances that beneficiaries receiving  such assistance , particularly in terms of financial 
benefits , are duly qualified and entitled  to rec eive such benefits.  The AE will need to exercise its 
policies and procedures  to ensure that benefits receive d or used in the project are not used for 
illicit purposes such as money  laundering , terrorist financing or other illicit purposes. The AE 
has such policies and procedures in place and will need to ensure that its implementing  
partners also apply those policies and procedures during the course of  the project. However , in 
reviewing the proposal , it does not appear that the activities will constitute an unusually  high 
risk and that , in view  of the commitments  of the AE , the compliance risk is determined to be 
medium .

4.4 Fiduciary  
57. The AE for the project is IFAD and , as such , the IFAD operational model ensures 
continuous implementation support and supervision of project activities. The IFAD  Manual of 
Administrative , Financial and Accounting Procedures , which has been validated by the 
Government of Madagascar and IFAD , will be complied with. The EE for the project is the 
Government of Madagascar through MEF and MINAE.  
58. As AE , IFAD will enter into a funded activity agreement with GCF on the contribution of 
the GCF loan and grant proceeds to IFAD , for which funds will flow into a dedicated bank 
account held by IFAD , while in parallel a subsequent subsidiary agreement between IFAD and 
the Government of Madagascar will outline the basis on which GCF proceeds (i) will be provided 
(on-lent/on -granted) to Madagascar in the agreed currency (i.e. U nited States dollars ) and (ii) 
will flow from IFAD to the borrower (MEF) into a project designated account in U nited States 
dollars opened at the Central Bank of Madagascar .  
59. As for the EE , a legally binding financing agreement ( subsidiary agreement) will detail 
the specific roles and responsibilities of the relevant government ministries , MEF and MINAE , 
with regard to  the implementation of the project. The subsidiary agreement will establish the 
terms for the financing and implementation of outcomes , outputs and activities , and include the 
relevant obligations of the E E in respect of the implementation of the overall funded activity , 
including compliance with the eligibility criteria and the project implementation manual. The E E 
through MINAE will select the beneficiaries of all activities of the project and procure service 
providers for specific project activities in accordance with the eligibility criteria and the project 
implementation manua l. The E E through MINAE will enter into contracts for procured services 
with the selected service providers.  
60. The project will have a n NSC to oversee the activities of DEFIS+ at the national level. The 
NSC will meet twice a year and its main role is to validate DEFIS+ annual workplans and budget; 
evaluate progress made on project activities , their results and their alignment with strategies 
for rural areas ; and manage project -related grievances that cannot be resolved at the regional 
level. Complementing the NSC , the project will have a project coordination unit to coordinate 
and manage DEFIS+ resources and activit ies in accordance with the financing agreement , 
project manuals on operations , financial management and administration procedures , 
monitoring and evaluation , and annual workplans and budget s, under the supervision of 
regional steering and monitoring committees , the NSC and IFAD. The project coordination unit


---
*Page 114*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 114 
 
 
will be responsible for (i) manag ing the project’s human , financial and physical resources; (ii) 
prepar ing annual workplans and budget s and project procurement plans; (iii) coordinat ing the 
implementation of the activities; (iv) monitoring and evaluation of the project’s activities and 
results; (v) coordinati ng and monitoring of the work/activities of partners and service 
providers; (vii) reporting and submitting activity and financial reports to MEF , MINAE and IFAD 
on a regular basis (minimum of twice a year in the case of IFAD so as  to fulfil its semi -annual 
reporting obligation vis -à-vis GCF); (viii) liaising regularly with regional government authorities 
and decentrali zed services and other actors in the DEFIS+ intervention zones;  and (ix) ensuring 
compliance with IFAD safeguards (i.e. environmental and social management plan , gender 
action plan) during project implementation activities. Planning and coordination of activities at 
the regional level will be provided by two interregional coordination units located in 
Fianarantsoa for the Ihorombe , Haute Matsiatra and Amoron ’i Mania regions; and Manakara for 
the Vatovavy , Fitovinany and Atsimo -Atsinanana regions.  
61. Joint supervision of the project will be provided by the Government of Madagascar 
(Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock ( MINAE ), Ministry of Economic and Finance ( MEF ), 
National Designated Authority (MEDD  NDA) and ONE) and IFAD . Supervision missions will be 
carried out twice a year and their main goals will be to (i) assess progress on the 
implementation of each DEFIS+ component; (ii) evaluate progress on the expected outcomes 
and outputs for each project component in comparison with what had been established in the 
concept note or other project documents; (iii) assess the quality of targeting and gender 
equality; (iv) analyse the different fiduciary aspects of the project (funding allocations , payment 
and disbursement commitments , conformity of expenditures with annual workplans and 
budget s and concept note , accounting records) and review outcomes of the external audits 
conducted in accordance with the IFAD Handbook for Financial Reporting and Auditing  for 
IFAD -Financed Projects . A mid -term review will be held to evaluate the actions taken in relation 
to the project’s objectives , analyse the effectiveness and approp riateness of the methodologies 
used , verify the level of disbursement of resources and determine the need to reallocate 
available resources.  
62. A no-objection letter from IFAD will be required for the approval of annual workplans 
and budgets , which will be screened to ensure that project activities build towards successful 
project completion in an effective and efficient manner. GCF funds will flow into a dedicated 
bank account held by IFAD that will be opened for DEFIS+ resources. This GCF account will hold 
GCF proceeds in trust and will be U nited States dollars  denominated and interest bearing. On -
lent loans will be in the same currency of disbursement and repaid in such currency , which will 
be United States dollars .  
63. The project’s accounts will be verified annually through external audits conducted by 
independent and qualified auditing firms in accordance with the IFAD Handbook for Financial 
Reporting and Auditing  for IFAD -Financed Projects  and in compliance with the applicable GCF 
requirements (i.e. expenditure eligibility , categori zation vis -à-vis GCF budget categories , etc.). A 
certified copy of the auditor’s report will be submitted to IFAD a few weeks before its deadline 
for sending such a report to GCF . 
64. It is recommended that condition s relating to the provision of a capacity assessment of 
the EE  in form and substance that is satisfactory to the Secretariat be reflected in the term sheet 
and funded activity agreement  as a placeholder.

65. As a  100  per cent  adaptation  initiative , the project is expected to benefit 447,201  direct

assumptions for defining both direct and indirect beneficiaries.   
66. The theory of change diagram went through a few rounds of revision and now 
adequately captures different levels of expected changes. Adaptation outcomes are shown to 
clearly represent outcomes. The theory of change  diagram identifies the co-benefits that  can be 
achieved and captures the causal relationships between outcomes and co -benefits. The barriers 
and risks that may hinder the achievement of these changes are identified and linked to

project activities , and how those contribute to the outcomes and impacts of the pro ject. Relevant

framework /performance measurement framework  and the programme  theory  of change.  The

4.6 Legal  assessment  
72. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 24 
September 2018 (the “ AMA ”), and it became effective on 9 November 2018. The Accredited 
Entity’s five -year accreditation term lapsed on 8 November 2023 but was extended by Decision 
B.37/18, paragraph (q) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the five -year 
accr editation term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the 
Board. The Board approved the re -accreditation of the Accredited Entity pursuant to Decision 
B.37/18, and negotiation of the Amended and Restated Accreditation Master Agreement is 
ongoing.  
73. The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has 
obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.


---
*Page 116*
---

 
GCF/B.3 8/02/Add. 07 
Page 116 
 
 
74. The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Madagascar, a country in 
which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other 
things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need 
to be further assessed. The GCF provided an updated draft agreement on privileges and 
immunities to the Government of the Republic of Madagascar on 13 May 2019.  
75. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity 
Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress 
activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is n ot provided with 
relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the 
GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and 
expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.  
76. To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by 
the Board is made subject to the following conditions:   
(a) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form 
and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, 
confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approv als needed by 
it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed project;  
(b) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the 
GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the 
Accredited Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has 
obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and  
(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.
